- (da) mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, let me first of all thank mr rapkay for the quality of his report and for the good cooperation that has been established. commissioner monti, i thank you for the excellent cooperation we have been able to establish between us and i want you to know how important your role is at the beginning of the new millennium. you have to sweep up after national governments with their ambitious competition policy, but only imagination can set limits to the accidents that national governments can cause. i will cite the latest examples we have heard of: holzmann, a company which receives considerable aid from the german government, sawmills in the former east germany and, above all, aid to shipyards. these are three sectors in which many danish companies are experiencing serious difficulties and losing market shares. let me say to mr poos that i fully share the view expressed by mr duisenberg that these are real cases of some eu member states failing to restructure their economies and thereby undermining the value of the euro. the liberal group tabled 80 amendments in the committee and they all concern state aid. these are amendments which we believe bring transparency and clarity, which are very important elements for the proper functioning of the internal market. i should like to take this opportunity to thank my colleagues on the committee on economic and monetary affairs for their support for the amendments tabled by the liberal group. as i have said, our amendments are about transparency and i would like to emphasize the amendment calling on the commission to present identical criteria and conditions for this type of state aid which we consider to be legal, since it is intended to guarantee the possibility for companies to foresee their situation. another question arises: what do we do when state aid is declared illegal? how can we be sure that this illegal state aid will be reimbursed? there is currently no common rule in this area and we strongly urge the commission to ensure that the rules on reimbursement are harmonised. this is the way forward if we are to guarantee a level playing field. finally, we propose the creation of a register - and many colleagues have spoken about this - but also the establishment of a scoreboard showing the current state of affairs in the countries in terms of state aid. you have shown us the way, mr monti, with a table of results on the internal market. this is what gave us the idea of proposing the same table for state aid. i hope very much, commissioner monti, that you will support these proposals and i look forward to hearing your comments and your position. finally, i would like to congratulate the commission on its xxviiith report on competition; once again, she did a good job. but i repeat: our priority must be to increase transparency and openness. the sectors i have mentioned need to be given a boost; the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the transparency of state aid. this is particularly necessary in relation to future enlargement and i would like to thank mr jonckheer for having examined in great detail in his report the problems connected with enlargement and how we can ensure that these countries will meet our requirements, but also how we will ensure a level playing field. it is obvious that the liberals and the greens have different ideas about how the world should be represented, but our aims are very similar and we will try to find a reasonable solution to our problems.
- (da) mr president, could a european prosecutor make the fraud against community funds disappear in football circles? i don't think he did. the existing instruments, however, allow us to go further. eurojust - the instrument proposed at the last summit - could be an alternative to the european prosecutor. eurojust should be the equivalent of europol in terms of its organisation and should be able to support the investigation of criminal cases. this is precisely the kind of practical cooperation we need. olaf, europol and the convention on extradition and mutual legal assistance are instruments which should be fully exploited and we will have taken a further step when the 1995 fraud convention is finally ratified by the member states. i agree with the rapporteur, however, that it is absolutely unacceptable that most member states have still not ratified it. this is unacceptable and i understand the impatience of those who are demanding that these instruments be replaced by a common european prosecutor. but it would be a huge, huge, giant thing to build. only the most serious cases would be subject to criminal sanctions; 90% of cases are disciplinary in nature and involve negligence or incompetence. what we need instead are effective internal controls and better redundancy opportunities. we must change the staff regulations and the disciplinary procedure, but above all we must change the way we operate. gross negligence still prevents the use of article 52 of the staff regulations on dismissals. should we not first sweep our own front door properly before embarking on projects to create brand new institutions?
commission - (el) under the danish pension scheme, known as "efterln", only those who reside in denmark and have completed the required periods of work in that country can receive a pension. workers complained to the european commission that they were not entitled to a pension. the danish authorities do not consider that they are obliged by community legislation to pay these benefits to workers in early retirement when they do not meet the conditions laid down by danish legislation. it should be noted that the regulation in force on social security systems does not at present mention early retirement issues, and the commission has proposed that certain amendments be made to that regulation, but these issues remain pending before the council. to date, there is no case law of the european court of justice on the "efterln", but it would be reasonable to argue that the residence conditions are incompatible with the general clauses currently in force on the prohibition of discrimination on grounds of nationality. the european commission services have initiated a process of joint meetings and meetings with the danish government with a view to finding a common language and treatment. the last meeting on this issue was held in november 1999 and we are awaiting the final proposals from the services as to whether an infringement procedure will be initiated against denmark.
- (da) i note that the commissioner did not directly answer my question as to the extent to which the commission approves the danish early retirement scheme as a whole, but i infer from this that the answer is in the negative. i would ask the commissioner to confirm expressly that the commission does not approve of this scheme. the commissioner went so far as to suggest that there was a strong case for bringing the whole issue of the danish early retirement scheme before the luxembourg court of justice on the basis of the existence of de facto discrimination against non-danish employees who cannot meet the conditions imposed because they have not been employed for the period required by the danish system. i would ask the commissioner if she could give us an approximate timetable in this case, because this is a problem that we must try to solve in the context of the socio-political debate in denmark. i would be grateful if the commissioner could look into this matter further.
commission. - (el) the european union directive on the posting of workers states that the mandatory rules governing the terms and conditions of employment which are in force in the host country must also apply to workers who have been posted to that country. the directive provides for two possibilities: either the legislation of the host country or collective agreements which have been established as rules of general application for a given branch. since denmark has no system of collective agreements with general application, the implementing decree must provide that, in addition to the existing working conditions laid down by law, the provisions of collective agreements which are of general application and have been concluded by the most representative organizations are valid for posted workers. in other words, denmark has two options: either to adopt legislation or, again by legislative means, to choose a collective agreement which will have legal force. discussions are taking place between the european commission and denmark, and denmark is expected to announce the transposition of this directive into its national law. the deadline for replying to the commission's questions expired on 6 december 1999. we don't have an answer. we're waiting to see what the next steps are.
- (da) i thank you for your precise answer. if the interpretation is correct, this means that the posting of workers directive entails an obligation for the danish state to establish a system of general conventions. your answer is very clear, but it is also - and i must emphasize this - a response which puts the danish trade unions, the danish government and the danish parliament in a very difficult political situation, because everyone knows that there are fundamental problems in the correlation between the danish model, which is largely based on collective agreements, and the continental model, which is based on legislation. the discrepancy and correspondence to which you refer, madam commissioner, concerns above all another directive, the working time directive, but we can therefore expect a new letter of formal notice and further legal action for breach of the treaty, the danish government having expressly notified its intention not to enforce the legislation and to introduce conventions of general application.

commission - (de) mr president, madam rapporteur, ladies and gentlemen, to the question whether we deserve discharge, i hope i can answer in the affirmative. in addition to the court of auditors' report, the discharge procedure for the budget contains all the information enabling the taxpayer to know whether - and to what extent - budgetary resources have been used sparingly and in accordance with political priorities, where errors remained and, above all, what steps have been taken to remedy them. public opinion and the commission are well aware that the discharge procedure is not a routine exercise but an act taken very seriously by parliament. it was the refusal of discharge for 1996 that led the previous commission to resign. with the debate we are having today on the discharge for the 1997 budget, a long process is therefore finally coming to an end. it dealt with virtually all the important subjects for which the commission is responsible. the report by mrs van der laan deals with all these instruments. this report is very ambitious. it focuses on reforms that have been introduced, but especially on those that have yet to be introduced. the commission shares 2000. the commission has promised parliament that it will make rapid progress in this area. the most important role in bat is played by the field of external policy. at the end of last year, my other requirements in this report are aimed at enhancing transparency. i would like to assure you that you are addressing an objective to which the commission is committed. the current commission improvements in areas where the member states decide on the resources allocated to the european community. i also support this recommendation and will ensure that it is applied in the areas where the - (da) mr president, i should like to thank the rapporteur for the quality of his report. i hope that the commission will take this as a basis for its reform work. the reform process has been under way for some time and there seems to be a kind of state of emergency within the commission. it is clear that the way the commission is managing things is not particularly good. there are of course capable and competent officials in the commission; they are the majority, but we need radical reforms. there's too little action and too much red tape. people must be given specific decision-making skills and they must be held accountable for their decisions. the financial regulation must be amended. we all agree that we need to control our spending more. the question is simply how. the commission and the committee of experts advocate the total abolition of advance checks. it's about being careful. we must maintain some form of advance control of expenditure. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the budget is properly managed. many irregular projects could escape the scrutiny of the authorities. we must, on the contrary, reform and decentralize controls. the commission does not have enough staff. as members of parliament we must have the courage to explain to our governments and our citizens that the resources available to the commission are not at all commensurate with the tasks which are its responsibility. and the commission must be allowed to refuse new tasks if the increase in staff does not follow. the system governing personnel is too rigid. there is a need for greater staff turnover, especially at the upper levels of the hierarchy. this will make it much easier to dismiss officials who show incompetence and incompetence. i therefore welcome the idea of reforming the disciplinary procedure. the disastrous examples of disciplinary cases so far have made it very clear that reforms are needed.
the commission is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter, and that it is not yet in a position to take a decision on the matter. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he has not already made a statement on this matter. she knows me well enough to know that in the five years we have worked together in this parliament, i have never refused to give a formal and detailed account of everything i have done. mr president, let me first of all pay tribute to the diligent and, as i have heard, painstaking work of mr van hulten in preparing this report. it is new to parliament but in some respects relatively old. as a former civil servant, he is well acquainted with the institutions - and i think his ability to deal with complex and extremely important subjects, as some members have pointed out, for all our institutions, confirms this. i thank him and wish him a long and distinguished career as a member of parliament. mr president, you are aware that mr van hulten's report on the second report of the committee of independent experts is necessarily long and, since i wish to give a full answer, particularly on matters relating to financial management and control, i will ask you to be lenient. i shall of course not take up more time in parliament than is absolutely necessary. when parliament considered the second report of the committee of experts last september, i promised on behalf of the new commission that it would be considered as a fundamental ingredient in the preparation of the commission's reform proposals. our efforts to meet our commitments will be evident to parliament when it sees the reform package that the commission has adopted today, in line with the demanding timetable set four months ago. i'm sure that will encourage mr. pomés ruiz. most of the proposals in this document are very close to those of mr van hulten and this document includes - as i told him and mr staes - a very explicit timetable of actions to be taken to bring about the reforms. the report i have had the honour to draw up is therefore not open or vague. the views of parliament during the consultation period of the next four to five weeks will be of great importance for obvious reasons. i would tell mr elles that we are definitely in listening mode. but he will admit - with his usual generosity, i am sure - that in order for us to be able to listen to your reactions to our proposals, we must first of all convey them to you. so we're in the transmission. although time prevents me, mr president, from commenting on all the elements of the resolution before parliament in this debate, i will be happy to ensure that attention is paid to detail in our proposals for reform throughout the consultation and in particular to our work in other important areas. i would like to make the following comments on the main themes of the resolution. the emphasis is rightly placed on the need for transparency, particularly because greater transparency in the commission's work will improve its effectiveness and demystify what the commission does. this is essential for an administration which must be accountable not only to this parliament but also to european public opinion in general. of course, safeguards are needed, particularly to protect particularly sensitive information, but the number of cases where confidentiality is necessary should be as limited as possible. i have stressed this point many times, not only as a commissioner but also as a politician, for 25 or 30 years. the commission is certainly intending to do so. it goes without saying that financial management and control are crucial areas for reform. as parliament knows and has often pointed out, the scale and scope of the union's financial interventions have increased considerably over the past decade, without a corresponding increase in human resources or adjustment of procedures. some members have mentioned this in this debate today. we share the view, strongly expressed by the committee of independent experts and reiterated in this draft resolution, that it is time for a thorough review of our rules and procedures. the means to achieve this are set out in the reform strategy and the commission will present its proposals for a radical overhaul of the financial regulation in april. in pursuing this fundamental objective, parliament's support will be absolutely vital. i agree with mrs theato when she points out that it would be totally unacceptable to make new arrangements without changing the legislation. it is essential to change the legislation. the reform strategy specifies and presents the preparations that can be made with all the guarantees, but it is clear that it is fundamental to enact this change in legislation for the system to work. essentially - and through changes in legislation - the commission will move away from the current centralised system of prior approval of all financial transactions by the financial controller and, instead, strengthen internal control systems within the expenditure departments so that directors-general are better equipped to assume their responsibility for decisions having an impact on the eu budget. in addition, in order to achieve greater guarantees than those offered by the present system, the new system of decentralized controls must be supplemented by the establishment of an internal audit service - the 'second key' - which, as herr bösch put it, is relatively compatible with the recommendations of the committee of experts. this service will be open from 1 may this year. it will be composed of qualified auditors and its independence must be and will be guaranteed by a new provision to be added to the financial regulation. the new department will be accountable to me and will be accompanied by an audit follow-up group chaired by my colleague, mrs schreyer, whom i have the pleasure of seeing in this house tonight. as commissioner for budgets, his responsibilities already include relations with the court of auditors. this audit monitoring group will ensure rigorous and effective monitoring of internal audits. i would like to stress very strongly that the modification of our control systems will in no way mean a relaxation. the proposed amendment aims to improve the efficiency of the systems, both in terms of inputs and in terms of measured and accountable results. i can also assure parliament that we are not moving towards what is sometimes called a 'big bang' approach. the prior financial control for each expenditure department will only be abandoned if the internal control system of a department proves to be entirely adequate. i am somewhat surprised that paragraph 10 of the draft resolution does not seem to take up the fundamental criticisms of the present function of centralised financial control expressed in the two reports of the committee of experts. this committee has clearly indicated the need to abolish the centralised prior visa. it was also clear that internal audit should be separated from financial control. i also think that paragraph 10 does not fully reflect the opinion of the court of auditors in 1997. the court will of course be able to give its opinion on the proposals for changes to the financial regulation, but it is useful to recall what mr karlsson said in this chamber last month: the commission's internal control, he said, was not strong enough to prevent incorrect operations. the financial controller has thus issued a positive prior authorisation in most cases of mismanagement or irregularity which have been discovered recently. at the same time, the internal audit function is not coordinated by certain bodies, in particular by the financial controller himself, by the financial inspectorate and by certain units managing general directorates. the original purpose of the centralised prior approval system was undoubtedly to ensure vigilance, but over the years it has had the perverse effect of reducing the extent of the sense of responsibility of managers in their decisions. i do not think there is any real disagreement between us on this. i gather from paragraph 10 that the underlying concern of members is that there should be a carefully managed transition. this will most certainly be the hallmark of change, as members will read in the reform strategy paper. our objective, that of the committee, that of the court of auditors, is not to abolish financial control, but to get rid of centralised financial control and to do better. before moving on to another area, i would add that we agree with the rapporteur that the existing internal audit function must be maintained until the new independent internal audit service is set up in a few months' time. i shall quickly turn to the section of the resolution on the fight against fraud, corruption, mismanagement and nepotism: the main new proposal in the report concerns denunciations of malfunctioning by commission officials. as i made clear at the hearings last september and on other occasions, we all agree that the mechanisms for this should be as well defined as possible, although we all hope, of course, that they will rarely be necessary. since last june, the olaf regulation has given staff better guidance on how to report possible irregularities. we propose to supplement these provisions by defining the rights and obligations of officials to report malfunctions they observe through internal channels but not exclusively through the same hierarchical line. the possibility of using specific external circuits will also be discussed. we try to put the best practices in place. a serious response to complaints, confidentiality at the initial stage and career protection will be guaranteed for those who report malfunctions in good faith and do not compromise investigations by untimely disclosures. corollary, there will be safeguards for public servants subject to false allegations. a communication to be published later in the year will give all the necessary details. i think that parliament will not be disappointed with our proposals. however, i am convinced by the suggestion in paragraph 34 of the report on the use of external bodies to apply the existing provisions on financial responsibility. we are already planning to reform the current disciplinary procedures to ensure thoroughness, honesty, consistency and professionalism. we will propose the establishment of an interinstitutional disciplinary council - that is another point for which we will need the support and understanding of parliament. a communication will specify the full change proposal in june. standards of public life is the next main section of the report presented to parliament. by introducing a series of codes of conduct, the present commission has begun to develop an explicit ethical framework. a further step will be taken with the proposal in june for an interinstitutional agreement on a committee on standards of public life. this is in line with the draft resolution. an important role for the committee will be to provide advice on ethics and standards and to supervise the institutions' own and joint codes of conduct. parliament's support on this point is welcome. we are also currently considering how best to implement the proposals on the classification system in paragraph 50 of the van hulten report. the draft resolution rightly recalls the commission's responsibility to this parliament. president prodi, vice-president de palacio and other colleagues have shown the practical commitment of the commission in this area. i hope that we can soon agree on a code of conduct for relations between our institutions, which will include an update of the rules on access to documents. mr elles and mrs thors have dealt with this problem very well. i am sure they know that in mid-december we officially received the draft framework agreement with parliament. we are now waiting for parliament to decide when it wishes to start negotiations on the text. we were looking forward to doing this as soon as possible. the report rightly stresses the central importance of human resources policy in the reform. i am pleased to say to mr van hulten and mr haarder that the detailed recommendations on recruitment, training, promotion and appointment to senior posts are in full agreement with our thinking and proposals. i also want to move towards a linear career system because the current system of grades is no longer adapted to the needs of our institutions. it is certainly blocking the advancement and mobility of people whose skills are confirmed. the comments made by members who, during this debate, rightly praised the commission's civil servants, the vast majority of whom are truly of great integrity, hard work and high competence, are absolutely correct. the white paper on the reform strategy will outline our views on these and other points, but of course the details and the precision are essential. a series of communications will therefore follow in the coming months. each of these will be subject to the reflections and reactions of this house. until then, it is clear that we will have to work closely with parliament as an institution on key issues of common interest, including pay and pensions and the revision of the staff regulations. on this last point, we are considering whether it is necessary to adopt a framework regulation laying down common provisions on essential subjects such as remuneration, conditions, staff representation rights, etc., but allowing the various institutions to implement rules on other points. the first ideas from parliament on this idea of consultation would be particularly welcome. in conclusion, i would refer to paragraph 15 of the parliament's resolution on the resource requirements of our policies for change. that's totally relevant. we are confident that we will receive a significant 'reform dividend' as the modernisation arrangements begin to generate greater efficiency and better management of resource use. it is nevertheless very clear that parts of the commission's services are already at the limit of their possibilities. parliament has frequently raised this question. secondly, it is also clear that the reform will require new investment in training and technology. thirdly, it is very clear that greater preparations are needed for enlargement. they already have implications for the availability of resources. it is also clear that if we are given new tasks - as the council and parliament will certainly do - we will have to identify the so-called 'negative priorities' and set them aside in order to ensure that we have room for manoeuvre. a key feature of the reform will therefore be to ensure a more rigorous process to link the priority setting process to the allocation of resources within an activity-based management system. i would like to stress, however, that although the discipline of setting priorities will certainly be introduced by the commission, it will only be fully effective if parliament and the council endorse it and adopt an equally rigorous attitude towards the requirements placed on the commission. the commission therefore welcomes paragraph 15 of the van hulten report. in conclusion, mr president, i would like to thank the house for its attention during this inevitably long speech and express my sincere gratitude to the rapporteur, the committee on budgetary control and the other committees which have given their opinions. we hope that we can work closely with parliament to complete the reform strategy package and then, and above all, work with parliament to implement it in a continuous and complex way over the years that will be needed to deal with such complex issues. - (da) madam president, we now have a timetable for more union, but what we need is a timetable for more democracy. portugal will present a draft programme for the intergovernmental conference on 10 february. the conference will open on 14 february. the foreign ministers will meet on 24 february and will meet monthly thereafter to discuss five important issues. firstly, there is the question of majority decisions. of the 65 areas where decisions are now taken unanimously, how many will be decided by majority voting, which will allow our ministers and civil servants working behind closed doors in brussels to further silence democracy in our member states? then there is the composition of the commission. each country will still be represented by a commissioner, but smaller countries will often get a smaller portfolio. thirdly, there is the weighting of votes in the council of ministers, the composition of parliament and the court of justice. the five main countries may benefit from a 25% increase in their votes in the council even if they lose the same number as a result of enlargement. fourthly, there will be increased cooperation. portugal, like other federalist countries, will press for a majority of member states to develop cooperation despite the opposition of some countries; we can see here a real abolition of the right of veto in important cases. fifthly, there is a remaining category, that of open to all proposals sent by the member states, and the programme may be extended, on the occasion of the porto summit, to the decisions taken at the helsinki summit. all this will lead to a new treaty on the union, which will replace the treaty of amsterdam before 2003. it will be adopted in nice on 8 december this year, but it will not necessarily be nice, unless the voters decide to shuffle the cards again. that is precisely what my group - the europe of democracies and differences - is asking for, and so is our intergroup sos democracy, which shares the same views, because what we need is more democracy and not more bureaucratic powers from brussels.
counsel. - (pt) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this debate has been of great use to the presidency and we will take note of and carefully examine the suggestions and comments that have been made. during this presidency we will be working a lot with the european parliament, the commission, high representative javier solana and the western european union, of which we are also the president. we hope that this cooperation will be fruitful in every sense of the word. we get the impression from this debate that there is a consensus on the basic guidelines of the portuguese presidency and that they are supported by part of the european parliament. this is important for us because it stimulates us to work with more consistency. as regards the question of employment and innovation, the extraordinary lisbon council found in parliament an extremely attentive assembly, interested and committed to making europe both a coherent social area and the most dynamic economic region in the world at the dawn of the new century. a europe capable of facing globalisation and of democratizing information technology by creating a knowledge-based economy free of the models and stigmas of an economy geared solely to production. the same is true of enlargement, the historic mark of the end of the cold war and the construction of a united europe. as for turkey, we will be working very closely on it to ensure that the verification of the acquis, the screening, the application of the copenhagen criteria and the strategic objective are working together, so that turkey can move closer to the european direction. i know that there are some doubts in this house about that. i would like to reassure them that a different policy would be even worse for europe, it would be very negative, it would have serious consequences for what we want to do as a union, both internally and also in the international context. as regards the intergovernmental conference, i think that it was possible to create a consensus of confidence between the presidency and parliament on the need to involve parliament in a more substantial, more genuine and more continuous way in the negotiating process. we're open for business. in my letter to the president of parliament at the beginning of the portuguese presidency, i made it clear, among other things, that we, as the presidency, were prepared to take part in the meetings of your committee on constitutional affairs, or even in the plenary sessions, if the european parliament wished to have a full debate on the subjects to be discussed at the intergovernmental conference. this would respect the separation of powers and the responsibilities of each institution, but i think it would stimulate the european debate on institutional reform. we have no inhibitions about these types of options. nor do we have a fixed view of the helsinki agenda. the commission's own words allow for the inclusion of new points, which i discussed with my colleagues during my tour of the european capitals. it is now the turn of the secretary of state for european affairs to do so. at the beginning of january i wrote to my european colleagues, the foreign ministers, asking them to indicate what items their respective governments thought might be on the leftovers agenda, so that we could have a clear view of this problem very early on, in the exercise of the presidency and the intergovernmental conference, which we could also share with the european parliament. i have also written to my colleagues in the applicant countries, those in the first and second waves of enlargement - which we would like to see one day forming a single wave - asking them to express their views on the intergovernmental conference, because the worst thing for the enlargement of europe would be if this institutional reform were perceived in central and eastern europe as a kind of preventive measure against newcomers. it would create a distrust relationship. the measures which the conference will have to adopt are measures which will make europe work better, and they are certainly not measures which are intended to prevent the countries which will one day join the union from having their voices heard in the european decision-making system. it is therefore important that they are involved now and that their views can be heard and understood. it is therefore not unrealistic, as one member has said, to think that we could have a clearer idea at the lisbon european council, provided there is a consensus on the scope and the framework of the intergovernmental conference's agenda. as far as the common security and defence policy is concerned, this is a dimension we are lacking in order to assert europe externally. the european union's foreign policy will be nothing without this component. in the next six months we will take important steps in this direction, we will have the first exercise in which nato will transfer capabilities to the western european union, which will act in accordance with the political orientation of the european union. but let our colleagues and those members who are not members of the atlantic alliance rest assured that this exercise will always have the effect of increasing european security, not reducing it. this will always involve a very attentive and very judicious relationship with those of us who are not in the atlantic alliance, but who will be able to access all these systems whenever they want to participate. if they decide to do so, they will act on an absolute level playing field with the other partners. as regards certain foreign policy points raised by the honourable members in relation to the european union/africa summit, we did not include this as an express objective of the portuguese presidency because when we took over the presidency we considered that this objective was not sufficiently consolidated and we did not want to frustrate the europeans by introducing a point on which we had no guarantees whatsoever. our working logic is as follows: we continue to take action so that we can retrieve this idea at any time, if it is likely to be retrieved in good time and in logistical conditions so that it can materialize. if not, we will continue to deepen the idea with our african partners and leave a foundation from which it can be taken up and developed in the future. as regards the question of the arms embargo on indonesia, in september, at the most dramatic moment in the situation in east timor, the european union decided to impose an arms embargo on indonesia for a limited period ending in january. that resolution did not contain any mechanism for automatic renewal. the consensus emerging in the council is that no new arms embargo should be imposed on indonesia for the time being, but that it should be made clear that the member states of the european union are subject to a strict code of conduct on this matter, which sets out very strict rules on the final destinations of arms and their use. we agree that this issue is still under consideration and that the european union has the embargo mechanism at its disposal at all times, as it has been. the embargo can be used for action; it can be used and used to correct and contain any dramatic situation. as for angola, it is a country with immense resources and a rare african characteristic: it does not have a large population, either in relation to its size or in relation to its resources. the situation in angola has led to conflict, instability, a situation of severe food shortages and great humanitarian concern. one of the parties to the lusaka accords has not respected them and has refused to surrender its arms, to transform itself into a civilian party and to return to the normal institutional game. this must stop, and the european union's position is to encourage all those in the unita camp who are willing and able to take political action and not resort to arms. everyone recognizes some needs. i was in angola about four days ago and spoke not only with the president and the members of the government but also with members of parliament from all quarters, including those who we can now consider to represent the various sensitivities of unita, and i was able to conclude that a large majority is in favour of improving the angolan system. some improvements are just beginning and their implementation must be stimulated: improvement of the financial system, improvement of national accounts, their transparency and their rules. in this connection, the start of negotiations with the international monetary fund and the possible signing of a first agreement shortly would certainly create the conditions for the establishment, within the framework of the paris club, of a multilateral mechanism to support the settlement of angola's debt, which would make it possible to release in the european union the funds granted several years ago in the framework of the round table on angola, but which are still held back for the reasons we know. the improvement of the institutional system, with a revision of the constitution, the debate of which naturally begins with the acceptance of the values of pluralism, the rule of law and freedom of the press. i would like to stress the importance of the debate in the angolan parliament on the rights of journalists and the fundamental need for an independent press in a modern political system. it goes without saying that this will promote angola's new position in the international system, an improvement in the united nations system, which is extremely important. i would point out in this connection that the angolan government and the secretary-general of the united nations have concluded their negotiations on the opening of a united nations office in angola with the task of accompanying and involving humanitarian aid, which will undoubtedly have important consequences for the angolan population. the need to demilitarise a political party is therefore simultaneously present and, moreover, to improve the institutions, the financial system and the system of articulation with the general framework of the united nations organizations. the european union is obviously very much involved in this process, both in the context of immediate humanitarian aid and in the development of a programme of support for reconstruction and rehabilitation. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is absolutely impossible for me, for reasons of time and because of the inclusion of a new item on your agenda concerning the middle east, to continue my speech, but i would like to conclude by saying that the presidency is very pleased to note that it is possible in this debate to be in general symbiosis with the fundamental lines we have proposed and to note that we have been able - and i believe we have succeeded - to define a method of working with the european parliament which will contribute greatly, i am sure and certain, to the enrichment of the council and parliament in the context of the general functioning of our institutions. (parliament adopted the resolution)
counsel. - (pt) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i will say very briefly that i have just returned with the high representative for foreign affairs, mr javier solana, the european union's special envoy, mr miguel moratinos, and the representative of one of the commissioners, from a trip to the middle east during which we had the opportunity, on behalf of the union, to meet the highest syrian, israeli, palestinian, jordanian, egyptian and lebanese figures, as well as the secretary-general of the arab league. the middle east peace process is well under way, but it faces two immediate obstacles. the first obstacle concerns the israeli-palestinian component, which is linked to certain delays in the timetable and the postponement of the date for the start of final status negotiations. the second obstacle is syria, with delays resulting from the refusal of the syrian side to continue negotiations in washington for the time being. the delay in the syrian issue is extremely important in that it makes it difficult and a precondition for the opening of negotiations on the lebanese issue. the european union has been involved gradually and very consistently over the years, through the work of the special envoy, mr miguel moratinos, whose work must be highlighted; the commission has been working on this issue for some time, through the continuing action of the commission and now the high representative. this visit will be a continuation of the visit to washington, where we will be receiving president arafat next monday at the general affairs council. the same way we welcomed the israeli foreign minister, mr israel levi. we are planning to meet the syrian foreign minister soon as part of the continuation of the negotiations. we will also be taking part - the presidency, commissioner christopher patten and the high representative - in the multilateral meeting in moscow on 1 february, where we will present the european union's positions. the union, without being a party to the negotiations, has undertaken to support the consolidation of the peace process through continued action, has been strongly present in the multilateral process and will now have the opportunity in february to relaunch the functioning of the working group on economic and regional development chaired by the union; it has of course been present, through the mediterranean partnership, in the elaboration of a series of association agreements with the countries of the region, financial assistance programmes and through the european investment bank. all the countries are aware - and we were able to see this during our visit - that, although not part of the negotiating process, despite our status as the main trading partner of virtually all the countries in the region and the main donor of official aid to the palestinian territories, the union has, in the medium and long term, an irreplaceable role to play in consolidating peace in the middle east. the appropriate frameworks are already in place to provide the true nature of this cooperation, both in aid to refugees, in the elaboration of integrated cooperation programmes, particularly with regard to the water problem, integrated water management, in relation to specific regional development programmes. it was also possible to ensure that our participation in this process did not come into conflict with the united states, but took place through the regular exchange of views. our mission, which ended yesterday, can conclude that the union's already real commitment to the whole middle east issue needs to be strengthened. this is another area where it is absolutely necessary to maintain permanent cooperation between the council and the commission, and between these two institutions and parliament. - (de) madam president, i have a request to make. paragraph 48 deals with a very sensitive subject, namely the membership of commissioners in a political party. since the vote on the first and second parts could be misinterpreted, since it is not a question of the first and second parts, but of an intermediate sentence, i ask you to read this intermediate sentence so that all colleagues know what they are voting on today.
in writing. - (da) we have decided today to vote against the creation of a european public prosecutor for the following reasons: we are not in favour of the existence of a common european criminal law or criminal authority. we believe that crime can be tackled just as effectively with existing instruments, such as europol and the extradition and mutual legal assistance conventions. in reality, the differences in criminal law between member states are not large enough to justify the use of instruments other than those currently existing between member states. we fully agree with the criticism of the 10 member states which have not yet ratified the 1995 fraud convention. we do not think that there is a legal basis in the treaty for the creation of a european public prosecutor. the treaty does not provide for the introduction of an authority governing the criminal law or the judicial system of the member states. eurojust, which was proposed at the tampere summit, is an alternative to the european public prosecutor. it would be a coordinated unit, composed of national prosecutors, to support the investigation of criminal cases. we welcome this initiative, because this is precisely the kind of practical cooperation we need. the european public prosecutor would initially deal only with crimes committed by european officials. in reality, what is needed is a fundamental reform of the staff regulations and the disciplinary procedure. only the most serious cases will be subject to criminal sanctions; 90% of cases will be disciplinary and will be about negligence or incompetence. what we need instead are adequate internal control procedures and a review of the immunity granted to civil servants.
in writing. - (da) we voted today in favour of the van hulten report because it contains many interesting proposals for reforms by the commission. we particularly call for a radical reform of the personnel policy and for greater efficiency and decentralisation of financial control. we have decided to vote against certain points. we voted against the creation of a european public prosecutor. we believe that a broader cooperation between states in criminal investigation, as proposed at the tampere summit, is a better idea. the staff regulations should be reformed, since only a limited number of cases have criminal consequences. we voted against the proposal to abolish advance checks altogether. some form of advance checks should be maintained. it is not enough to carry out unannounced checks when the money has been spent. on the contrary, controls must be reformed and decentralised. we voted against the proposal to restrict parliament's access to information. parliament must have the right to inspect all the documents which the commission makes available to the committee of experts. we also voted against the creation of more expert committees. the relevant parliamentary committees can very well carry out studies and put forward proposals for reforms. the creation of expert committees is dangerous: it would undermine the role of parliament as an institution elected by universal suffrage.
- (da) the two oral questions we are considering, which are worded in the same terms, are based on a mantra which has increasingly turned politics in recent years into a kind of ideology - harmonious but fatal. the european mantra today is called the epsj - area of freedom, security and justice. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption by the end of the year. are there people who are not in favour of the ideas of security, freedom and justice? the problem is simply that these are not concepts which the eu can introduce through legislative decrees or other supranational resolutions. freedom, security and justice are the basic system of a society. these concepts are a reflection of the history of each society, its social experiences and its political evolution. these are not concepts that the eu can introduce in a way that is harmless to society. and it is precisely here that we find the real rational element of the mantra "security, freedom and justice". the aim is not to guarantee justice to citizens. they already have this guarantee in the national judicial systems. the aim is to transfer essential elements of criminal law, criminal policy and the administration of justice from one society to the eu hegemony. the aim is to strengthen integration which, under the guise of a deceptive packaging, will lead to more repression and control. remember all the decisions made about fortress europe, schengen, eurodac, etc. any democrat can see two key problems. firstly, the initiatives proposed are totally unrealistic. how can we imagine that the european institutions, already crumbling under the weight of their ambitions, could achieve these ambitious projects? remember the strong criticism last year of the commission for its dishonesty, lack of ethics and lack of any sense of responsibility. the oral question also contains indirect phrases. on the other hand, these projects are overwhelming, if not paralysing, for national democracies. as long as the eu was concerned with the internal market, only its body was attacked; today, we're going after his soul.
counsel. - (pt) mr president, i should like to begin by saying that i do not wish to make any personal comments such as those with which you began your second question, and i would be grateful if you would refrain from making any such comments in future. as regards the substance of your question, we get the impression that the criticism of the council, apart from its specific form, which we also take note of, does not refer to the opacity of, say, the legislative process, but almost to an excess of transparency. i would like to say to you, mr president, that the results of this kind of transparency process are clear to us and beyond doubt. we will not hesitate to repeat this information in writing to you, mr president, but we cannot go beyond what we have said because it is exactly what the treaty requires. we therefore believe that all the information which the general secretariat of the council is now making available to the public is essential to the way in which the council functions as a legislative authority.
counsel. - (pt) mr president, the answer given is the only answer possible. i would add that the interpretation of article 255 of the treaty must also be linked to the principle of subsidiarity. at the moment, there are no concrete proposals to enable us to work on the regulation of this article. without this proposal, no progress can be made in this area. in any case, the first reading we make is that article 255 does not allow work to be done on a basis which could affect the principle of subsidiarity.

- (da) mr president, let me begin by congratulating mr cunha on his brilliant report, which we can, on the whole, support. we are very concerned about the remarks made by the commission in its annual report on the results of the multiannual development programmes for the fishing fleets in the community. it should also be noted that the report is from 1997 and that we are now in the year 2000. the annual report is characterised by a number of changes to the base and therefore by complex estimates of the measures, which are all the less robust as not all member states comply with the programmes. some countries are achieving the final objectives for map iv, while others are still hesitant or significantly behind schedule. other countries have not provided reliable data. it is important to point out the possible existence of serious inaccuracies in the absence of homogeneous comparison criteria. mr cunha points out in his report that the aim of the multiannual development programmes is to strike a balance between the available fisheries resources and the fishing effort of the community fleet. it stressed that sustainable development in this sector could only be guaranteed by a significant reduction in fishing fleets. in this connection, my concern is particularly directed at young people who are starting out and at the investments which have been made in new ships. the socioeconomic sacrifice must also be shared fairly and justly between the member states. we are concerned about the negligence - if not indifference - of some member states, who do not report reliable data on the situation of their fishing fleets. it is also very clear that a number of member states are not inclined to reduce the capacity of their fishing fleets in accordance with the objectives and decisions of the programmes. the committee on fisheries calls on the commission and the member states to adopt uniform and comparable criteria for the measures; this requirement must be respected. the commission is also asked to step up its checks on the development of the member states' fleets. this is also a point which we strongly support. as a follow-up to the reinforcement of controls, the commission is invited to implement measures - and, where appropriate, to reinforce those already implemented - to oblige member states to comply with the provisions on the reduction of fishing fleets. this may take the form of a refusal to provide aid for the renewal or modernisation of fishing fleets or of an action before the european court of justice. mr cunha suggested that insufficient data from the member states could lead to a reduction in fishing quotas. we do not think that this is the best way to proceed, because we do not think that it is the fishermen who should be punished for the negligence of the member states. we have asked for a separate vote on this amendment. we also asked for a separate vote on the reference to the principle of relative stability, because we believe that this is an amalgamation which could lead to a distortion of the principle laid down in the treaty.
- (da) mr president, i should like to thank the rapporteur for his report, the content of which i fully share. i also thank him for the collaboration that has been able to develop between the groups, which has enabled us to reach compromises which i believe are responsible, adequate and appropriate. our group will therefore approve them. why are we dealing with this problem? very briefly, it is about limiting man-made co2 emissions. we know that as asia benefits from increased living standards, 1.4 billion chinese and 1 billion indians will start to consume more and more energy; this means that their energy consumption, which currently represents 10% of the eu's average energy consumption, will increase sharply as they require basic amenities such as hot water and perhaps even air conditioning, transport and the modernisation of their industries. we made a commitment in rio to protect the climate. this document is well suited to this objective. we noted that electricity generation accounts for 30 percent of man-made co2 emissions. and electricity accounts for 35% of all energy consumption in the eu. long fluorescent linear tubes account for 53% of the eu's electricity consumption for lighting purposes. if we can meet our targets, we will have saved, by 2020, 6 million tons of co2 emissions a year. we will save 10% of electricity consumption for lighting purposes by using fluorescent tubes, which, translated into money, is a saving of 250 million euros per year. this proposal will also have considerable effects on the climate and the economy and, if we achieve the proposed objectives, our industry will have enough time to restructure. mr turmes has raised this point and i remember that the industry has known, at least since 1992, that magnetic ballasts will be more or less banned by 2009. so we are not introducing anything new. we have come together around the compromise that has been drawn up to improve energy efficiency in a bid to protect the climate. we wanted to obtain economic benefits and we wanted, by taking a gradual approach, not to burden ordinary consumers unnecessarily with investments that cannot be repaid. at the same time we have ensured that the northern regions of the union, northern finland, northern sweden, northern norway, greenland and other areas where electronic ballasts are not suitable can continue to use magnetic ballasts. it is an excellent proposal and i recommend that it be supported and adopted by all, as the groups have agreed.
in writing. - (da) we voted for the compromise proposal on the basis of the good intentions expressed. however, we must stress the importance of ensuring that the discussion on the introduction of a possible tobin tax takes place at a global level, in national and international forums, and that this does not lead, as a side effect, to an increase in the eu's powers to the detriment of these forums.
- (da) mr president, the president of the commission did not show much honesty when he spoke last week about the intergovernmental conference. he said that unanimity would still be needed on social systems of the member states can be changed by a majority decision taken in brussels, even if the french national assembly, the british house of commons and the dutch house of representatives are influence when they cast their ballot. voters will be deprived of this means of decision, of this possibility of changing things on election day. brussels is a better judge. mr prodi had announced the need for confidentiality on an individual basis against the requirements and expectations of citizens in terms of transparency. if the commission regulation is adopted, it will have the right to withhold certain categories of documents without having to examine certain specific elements. a right to confidentiality and non-disclosure of certain documents, which are now publicly available in a - (da) mr president, i am pleased to be able to share with you some striking observations from a small group of nordic members who are sceptical and opposed to the process of violent integration, which is gaining ground and which is threatening the very foundations of democracy in europe. i am thinking of the speeches by mr bonde and mr sjöstedt, who pointed out that the draft in question contains a number of initiatives, perhaps unintentional but certainly rational and authoritarian, towards the creation of a centralised and federalist eu. they have rightly linked this problem to those of monetary union and centralised management and have put forward some alternative democratic principles. i would add that any rational political agitation in my country, denmark, is based on the following idea: it is impossible to increase the number of eu member states excessively, and thus to enlarge the eu, and at the same time to pursue deeper integration, that is to say, to carry out more intensive qualitative integration in the direction of the creation of the united states of europe. and that is precisely what is happening. we have seen that each time the eu is enlarged geographically, it is always followed by an intensive deeper enlargement, and this is precisely what is envisaged in the motion for a resolution tabled by the committee, in particular in paragraph 7, which calls for deeper integration. in the light of the events of the last few days, one may wonder what the purpose of these intergovernmental conferences and of these treaty amendments will be when the heads of state or government - in reality the european council - take a stand against a sovereign member state by intervening in the democratic process of that member state. everyone is free to have their own opinions about mr haider - personally, i consider him a very dangerous political figure - but we do not have to interfere in the democratic process of a sovereign and friendly country. at the intergovernmental conferences and when we review the amendments to be made to the treaty, we will have to take into account the fact that the eu is changing every day, in particular, as we have just seen, in a way that is contrary to the treaty.
- (da) mr president, this directive is important in our efforts to prevent the accumulation of hazardous waste from end-of-life vehicles and it is also important in encouraging the recycling and recovery of materials from vehicles that have been disposed of. i therefore believe that it is essential, in the interests of environmental protection, to apply the ban on the use of toxic heavy metals in full and not to restrict the responsibility of producers. if we limit this responsibility, we will not encourage manufacturers to build and manufacture vehicles that produce less waste. the common position, adopted by the commission and subsequently confirmed by the committee on the environment, but which was very difficult to accept by the council, meets environmental requirements in every respect, so i find it very strange and incomprehensible that members of the committee on the environment have tabled amendments to reduce environmental requirements and to reduce the liability of car manufacturers. if these amendments are adopted, i believe that the credibility of the house on environmental matters will be seriously undermined. the house has so far played a positive role as a catalyst for environmental protection, but the adoption of the amendments by mr florenz and mr lange would be a negative factor for environmental protection in europe. on the other hand, if we mitigate the responsibility of producers in this case, this will have serious consequences in cases which we will examine later in other areas, for example with regard to the future directive on electronic and computer waste. i therefore strongly recommend that members disregard their membership of either group and vote against all amendments to the common position which seek to weaken environmental standards and restrict manufacturers' liability. this is how we will achieve an environmentally sound result.
in writing. - (da) the danish social democrats voted today against the report on the convening of the intergovernmental conference. we believe it is essential that this intergovernmental conference can be concluded before the end of the year 2000 in order to avoid that formal elements such as the weighting of votes, the composition of the commission and that of the european parliament become an obstacle to the enlargement of the eu. we therefore expressed our great satisfaction with the decisions taken in this connection at the helsinki summit in december. a programme that is far too ambitious would now risk delaying the enlargement process. that is not what we want and that is why we voted against the report. we are, however, in full agreement with our colleagues that the intergovernmental conference must be transparent so that the public can be informed of the progress of our work.
- (da) i have been personally involved for many years, both in words and in deeds, in the fight against fascism and xenophobia and in the fight for equal rights, but i cannot accept what has happened recently, first the concrete threats made by the council of ministers, then the threats made by mr prodi against the austrian commissioner and finally this resolution. i could not vote for this resolution despite the fact that i agree with many of the points made in the recitals, mainly because i see in it an incredible amalgam of power, arrogance and weakness on the part of the european union, which does not bode well. the adoptions today are not only contrary to the treaty and give the union's institutions more powers than they have, but more importantly, they will have the opposite effect. these sanctions will not weaken mr haider or the fpö, on the contrary, they will strengthen them. we'll get the exact opposite of what we're looking for. this is not the way to combat racism and the rise of the far right.
in writing. - (da) the danish liberal members of the european parliament insist that the adoption of this day does not in any way constitute parliament's support for the diplomatic sanctions decided upon by the 14 prime ministers against austria. that is why the danish liberal members of the house have supported the very strong rejection by the liberal members of any form of xenophobia in austria and elsewhere. we attach great importance to the possible use of the new provisions of the treaty of amsterdam which allow the exclusion from the right to vote in the eu council of ministers of any country which, by its actions, violates the fundamental rights of citizens through discrimination, etc. (article 7).

- (da) madam president, mr prodi, i welcome your proposal to radically decentralize the activities of the union, but i ask you why you are then presenting a set of centralizing laws. i remember the speech your predecessor gave five years ago. mr santer made the same promises as you - "less and better" - but what we got in the end was "much more and much worse". i don't think you're in a position to keep your promises. you talk about decentralization, but you act as a centralizer. this collection of laws is just a monotonous list of issues on which citizens will lose all influence; it is you, mr prodi, who are taking away that influence from the citizens, even in social matters. you talk about increasing transparency, but you put forward proposals to hide documents that are now accessible. your commission is made up of 20 people who are the only ones in the eu who can propose a reduction in the volume of legislation. no local politician has this power from the moment a law has been passed in brussels. the commission's compilation of laws should therefore be accompanied by at least an equally large compilation of powers which are devolved to the member states and to the democratic power of the citizens, otherwise the volume of legislation adopted in brussels will continue to grow. we have passed 10,000 laws and as many legislative amendments; the candidate countries received 26 000 documents, which for the polish parliament, for example, represent 140 000 pages to be examined. already today, that figure is far, far too high. the commission is proposing to reduce the number of decisions taken by brussels and to give more power in this area to citizens, regions and member states. the commission has already taken a number of decisions on the basis of the proposals contained in the white paper on growth and employment. now that you, mr prodi, and your predecessors have deprived the citizens of their right to self-determination, the quality of the work done in brussels must be significantly improved and that work must be carried out in full transparency so that the citizens at least have the impression that they are being heard. i would just like to make a comment on what mr dell'alba said about the dream of the founding fathers: read the memoirs of jean monnet. his dream was to create what he called a small, practical secretariat. it is certainly not this small secretariat that mr prodi is now chairing.

in writing. - (da) the european project began as a collaboration between states. this cooperation has ensured peace and stability in our part of europe for over 50 years. as a result of this economic and political success in our part of the world - and the eu is a prime example - and the ever-increasing expansion of our western values, competition in international markets has increased sharply in recent decades. the eu must be prepared to face this competition. this will only be possible through increased cooperation within the community. it is not a question of increasing the integration of countries in the form of a federation, but of exploiting our economic potential across borders. we must work together on common projects and pool our strengths from all sides. a programme such as interreg is precisely helping to support regional development projects across borders - projects to create centres of economic growth at european level, which will be able to meet the challenges of our international competitors. that is why i am in favour of continuing this programme.
- (da) mr president, first of all i should like to thank mrs lienemann for her report. it is here and now that we decide how much eu member states will have to work effectively to make the aquatic environment cleaner in the years ahead. as a parliament we must amend the council's common position in order to show the way forward towards a healthier environment. if this directive is not amended, the consequences could be very serious and long-lasting for the environment and the quality of drinking water. refusal to amend the directive would send a bad signal to european industry and to the european public. we must absolutely limit the total emissions of chemicals into our water areas. an individual analysis of the approximately 100 000 chemicals currently in circulation would take far too long. we can't afford to wait. i would ask the house to support amendment no 108, which seeks to replace the words 'pollution of water by various pollutants' with 'prevention of water pollution by continuous emission reduction'. we cannot delay our action on behalf of the environment by an excessive search for detail; we must work to reduce the overall emissions of hazardous substances into nature. the european environment ministers must remain true to their commitment to the ospar convention, which already defines substances dangerous to the environment. this convention must be the guiding principle for our actions in favour of a healthier environment, which we and our successors deserve.
- (da) mr president, the aim of this directive should be to ensure joint protection by the member states of groundwater, surface water and drinking water and a level of protection that corresponds to existing environmental legislation within the eu. let me remind you that not all member states have yet implemented previous decisions on the nitrates directive, despite the fact that it is a european directive. this proposal contains a number of measures to strengthen the protection of the aquatic environment and which go beyond our current environmental objectives. the paper proposes to achieve an end target for concentrations in the marine environment close to baseline values for natural substances and close to zero for man-made synthetic substances. it is not possible and it is contrary to nature. the adoption of this report will have serious consequences for european agriculture if the concentration of phosphorus and nitrogen, for example, cannot exceed the basic values for these substances in the aquatic environment and if a near-zero value is established for pesticides in the aquatic environment. for example, european farmers will no longer be able to produce high-protein bread wheat. this will result in the relocation of agricultural production to other countries outside the eu and will entail significant social and employment costs. i cannot accept the parts of the water directive which deal with these points.

social policy is based; however, a strong and competitive economy is the best guarantee of a good quality of social security. we are therefore glad that these issues are on the agenda. the term 'social convergence' is often used in discussions about the coordination of social policy within the eu. as liberal members of parliament, we accept the notion of social convergence, insofar as the eu is setting broad common objectives which the member states must strive to achieve through their national social policy. it is not a question of harmonization, but of common objectives. it is a fact that the eu member states organise their social policy in different ways. we have different traditions, different cultures and despite many similarities, the nature and extent of social problems also differ considerably from country to country. it is therefore important to emphasize that social policy is a national prerogative. the national policy is obviously subject to a number of common frameworks, such as the minimum social rights established by the treaty, the guarantee of free movement of workers without risk of losing their social rights, the coordination of economies and economic cooperation. coordination and cooperation are elements that must be added, but convergence must be based on objectives and not on means. i would also like to thank the rapporteur, mr jan andersson, for his very constructive efforts in trying to reconcile different points of view and thus secure support for his report. i shall vote in favour of his report, but there are a few points member states. i am sceptical about the detailed regulation that could result from the commission's intervention in relation to the extent and quality of childcare and care for the elderly and i am sceptical about the value of a common poverty line. the coordination of social policy and dialogue are good things, but we must avoid putting everyone in the same basket; we must maintain the plurality of solutions.

- (da) mr president, i too thank the council and the commission for their contributions and i join the other members in deploring the fact that cohesion policy is not given a more central place. we in parliament know why this is so. so i would like to start by giving you some general figures which i think are useful. at the beginning of the 19th century, the real per capita income in the richest countries of the world was three times that of the poorest countries. in 1900, it was 10 times higher, and in the year 2000, it's 60 times higher. there is no sign that this trend will be reversed - quite the contrary. the curve appears to be exponential and the differences will continue to widen, as shown by the following figures: the income gap between the richest fifth of the world and the poorest fifth was 30:1 in 1960, 60:1 in 1990 and 74:1 in 1997. these numbers predate the internet revolution. in other words, the development policy of the rich countries, including the eu, would have to make gigantic progress, otherwise the rich countries would have to build walls to protect themselves from a massive influx of immigrants. in a global village, the poorer parts of the village will no longer tolerate these huge differences. an insurrection is foreseeable and it will be justified. i would therefore ask the council to implement the announced debate as quickly as possible and, above all, to conclude it so that we can all, including european citizens, understand the implications of globalisation, in particular how radical the changes to the development sector will be. as far as our development aid and its level are concerned, we are far from the eu average of 0.7% promised. there's no point in knowing that others are doing worse. we must first of all open up the possibility of trade with other countries. i do not think i have heard the word 'trade' once in the speech of the president-in-office of the council -
