- (da) the edd group was not able to vote on the andersson report despite the fact that it contains some interesting ideas such as the recognition, on page 15 of the explanatory statement, that 'the differences between the member states in the degree of development and traditions in the field of social protection make genuine harmonization impossible'. the concept of 'genuine harmonization' is one of these confusing terms, which completely obscures the process of integration in question. it is also true, as the explanatory memorandum explains, that harmonisation would be detrimental in that it would impose higher requirements on the weaker member states, while competition reasons could lead the more socially developed states to make cuts in their social protection systems. such harmonization would not benefit anyone". the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the report stresses that the union can bring added value by implementing genuine, binding and effective convergence criteria and that this process of convergence must be developed through joint discussions within the ecofin council and the social affairs and employment councils. finally, point 7 emphasises that 'the process of social convergence is absolutely inseparable from effective and ambitious fiscal coordination'. we are now getting closer to the heart of the problem: this project of social harmonization is not a matter of social policy; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we therefore wish to stress that we cannot support this proposal in so far as it is aimed at approving the creation of emu.
in writing. - (da) the danish social democrats in the european parliament voted today in favour of the report by our swedish social democrat colleague, mr jan andersson, on the commission communication on a concerted strategy to modernise social protection. the commission's communication is based on the achievement of emu, which, by requiring stability and growth, will enable the member states to maintain their social security systems. the report states that modernisation does not mean a reduction in the level of social protection, but a better use of available resources. the explanatory memorandum also states - and it should be noted - that modernisation will not mean harmonisation of social protection systems either and that harmonisation would be detrimental in that it would impose higher requirements on the weaker member states, while competition reasons could lead other states to reduce their social benefits. this is a situation that would not benefit anyone. both the commission communication and the andersson report emphasize that it is the member states who are responsible and will therefore decide on the content of social security systems. collaboration in these areas within the eu framework will add value based on an exchange of experience, a mutual evaluation of policy development with a view to establishing best practices.

- (da) i would like to continue in the same vein as the previous speaker. there are a few questions of principle, and above all the fact that criminal law is a national prerogative. criminal law is a matter of cultural traditions in each society and it is very difficult to find common definitions of what is meant by these basic legal concepts. i pay tribute to the work done by mrs cederschiöld and i will base my speech on her demands in relation to the tampere summit, namely the development of common definitions and rules on matters of criminal law and the implementation of common sanctions. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union. however, these considerations have no legal basis, and this is quite normal, since criminal law is a matter for national jurisdiction. i would like to ask the commission and council representatives the following question: is the legal basis provided sufficient? in my view, this is clearly contrary to article 34 (2) (b), which defines the legal basis for 'framework decisions', which are binding on the member states as regards the objectives pursued, but in respect of which it is left to the national authorities to determine the form and means of implementation. the draft framework decision before us is typical in that it sets out in detail the obligations of the member states without leaving them any freedom to choose the means of implementation. i think that this is going beyond the legal framework. i look forward to a reply from the commission and the council, because it is clear to me that article 31 is not a legal basis.
- (da) it is undeniable that there are various interpretations of the di pietro report within my group. i agree with what has been said before, that this is good work, done with skill in difficult conditions. the danish members of the group will not vote for the proposal, but they will vote for a number of amendments, including those tabled by the greens. there are two reasons for our refusal. firstly, it is a terribly complex and sensitive legal issue and i believe that the proposal has certain risks to legal certainty which have not been recognised. this reason alone is sufficient to explain our refusal to vote on the proposal, but there is a second reason: even though it is a convention, that is to say a collaboration between states, the proposal shows very clear trends, namely the creation, in the longer term, of a common european judicial area, a common european criminal code and a common european administration of justice. we are opposed to this development, we are opposed to the mantra of 'an area of freedom, security and justice', which consists in the barbarous creation of community systems that are destroying certain legal traditions linked to these sensitive and important questions, which are above all national prerogatives.
in writing. - (da) the cederschiöld report reflects a very strange logic: the eu has decided to introduce a common currency, the risk of counterfeiting is increasing, counterfeiting of the euro is considered a crime against the whole of the eu so that the sanctions must be identical in all member states. criminal harmonization or the introduction of minimum legal standards should not be justified on the basis of problems created deliberately; these decisions must be justified on the basis of common sense and necessity. there is nothing to justify the dismantling of the legal principles, legal traditions and adequate legal systems of the member states by the existence of a crime as common as counterfeiting. in accordance with the international convention of 20 april 1929, there can be no confusion as to which country should examine a possible criminal case. we therefore vote against the report and strongly urge that the idea of criminal harmonization on such a weak basis be abandoned and that efforts be directed towards preventing this problem.
in writing. - (da) the danish social democrats in the european parliament voted today in favour of the report by mrs monica frassoni, on the basis of which the european parliament accepts the agreement reached with the commission on the detailed rules for the application of the council decision of 28 june 1999 on comitology. in any case, we attach great importance to the greatest possible transparency in decision-making procedures within the framework of community cooperation, particularly with a view to guaranteeing the indispensable democratic control. the agreement will enable the european parliament to receive automatically the relevant documents relating to cases which have been adopted under the provisions of article 251 of the treaty. we would have liked to see this regulation extended to other areas where the relevant parliamentary committees could receive the requested documents, after agreement. the approval of this agreement does not constitute an achievement in terms of transparency and democratic control of comitology procedures, but it represents real progress and we do not want to impede that progress.
- (da) mr president, the events taking place in mitrovica, kosovo, are appalling and unacceptable. violence begets violence and vengeance begets vengeance. the police force sent in is insufficient to prevent these massacres. the chief commissioner of police, mr. svend frederiksen, expressed his despair, saying that he still lacked more than half of the promised personnel. he has less than 2,000 agents when he was promised nearly 5,000. the police force must be as it appears in the landscape, the population must believe that it can stop the violations and it must be of such a scale that it is able to shed light on the crimes committed and punish the guilty, otherwise the door is open to self-justice. that is why we need to train local police officers, and it was planned that 2,000 to 3,000 police officers would be trained, but the police force still has only 200 officers who are trained and able to contribute to the investigation work. we have already seen, a year ago, what happens when agreements and decisions are not respected. i was in kosovo on october 27, 1998, when the serbs withdrew. it was agreed that 1,800 osce observers would be sent to ensure peace. a month later, there were only a handful of them. by christmas, there were perhaps 100 and by spring, there was still only a tiny fraction of the promised number. the violations could not even be noted. then the war came and the observers withdrew. so my question is this, commissioner: can you really do nothing to prevent a repetition of our stupidity in europe? can you not guarantee a police force of 5,000 men and the training of local agents, who would obviously be able to solve crimes more easily than foreign agents? i would like to ask you another question: when do you think they will be in place? and when will you delegate more responsibility to local democratic forces? i ask this last question because it is the solution we must consider as soon as possible if peace is to have a chance.
in writing. - (da) tourism is an important source of understanding between peoples and an important component of the labour market; the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. it is essential that europeans, like the rest of the world, have the opportunity to become aware of the different ways of life of each and every people and how each people is socially organized. tourism policy cannot therefore be shaped and defined by the european parliament or even by the eu as a whole. i shall therefore vote against the proposal.
- (da) mr president, mr solana, you have the full support and confidence of the liberal group, but we would like to remind you of the enormous responsibility that now rests on your shoulders. i am thinking, among other things, of humanitarian, reconstruction and peacekeeping operations. but i am also thinking of the responsibility which is now yours, to ensure that the new european defence cooperation does not create any division between nato and the eu, or between the usa and europe. such a division would only please despotes and rebellious states that threaten freedom and human rights. i think you heard the concerns expressed last week in this house by some members of the united states congress. you must dispel these concerns, mr solana, because, as you yourself have said, if we are united on both sides of the atlantic, we can do something. if we divide, we weaken ourselves and thus prolong the suffering of the oppressed. so, mr solana, maintain the closest contacts with our american allies, not to mention norway, turkey and canada. it is also largely thanks to them that we will not become paper tigers. we have, as mr george robertson has often said, two million soldiers, but we have not even been able to put 40,000 in kosovo. perhaps it was our weakness and lack of credibility that led to the war. diplomatic pressure is only effective when backed by credible economic and military threats. the rest is wishful thinking. member states must therefore either allocate more money or commit more to modern mobile units so that our soldiers have the best possible equipment to protect themselves; we shall then be able to rejoice in the decisions taken last monday in sintra. the third heavy burden on your shoulders is the guarantee of parliamentary control. it must first and foremost be exercised at the level of the member states. no soldier can be sent on a mission without the consent of the national parliament. so it is wrong to talk about a european army. it is a force composed of national units. what is new is that it is fast, well prepared and has an efficient joint command and modern equipment. there is also, of course, room for a certain right of scrutiny and control on the part of our parliament. you are often our guest, whether here in plenary or in the committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy, and we regard you as someone who is able to speak and listen. i would like to conclude by expressing my appreciation for the incredible unity of thought and the speed with which this new cooperation is being established. some of us met yesterday with the portuguese minister of defence. he was as delighted and positively surprised as you are to find that we were able to agree on so many things. remember, there are also countries not aligned around the table. we share your joy that we are not moving too fast to create a breach in transatlantic cooperation. this is something i would like to stress again, and i will stress at last that the aim of all this is to become so credible that when we apply economic and military pressure, military intervention never takes place. that is the purpose of all this, and it is therefore also important that you work in collaboration with mr patten, because it is the military, economic and diplomatic pressures within the community that will put a barrier to encroachments on the territories around us.

in writing. - recent progress has been made in increasing the number of women in the decision-making bodies of the union. in our own institution, 30% of the elected representatives are now women, including the president of the european parliament herself. finland also just elected a woman president. on average, almost a quarter of the governments of the member states and just over a fifth of their parliaments are made up of women. these rates are still far from satisfactory, especially as the figures vary widely from one member state to another: women in the nordic countries are much more represented in senior positions and in decision-making processes. this reflects more respect for the quality and competence of women's contribution to society than the result of fixed quotas. my group is not in favour of the use of quotas in general because in the long run they risk going against the purpose or even undermining the cause they are intended to support. we recognize, however, that experience varies from one member state to another and that some use of such measures is sometimes justified as a temporary remedy to rebalance the representation of women where they are clearly under-represented. we could support the resolution on women in decision-making, adopted to commemorate international women's day on 8 march, for which the liberal group tabled ten amendments and relaxed the reference to quotas in accordance with the approach advanced above.
- (da) madam president, i do not think mrs banotti should be so excited about being able to receive two dutch channels. it would be better to be able to pick up channels in all languages. i hope we can get a danish tv channel next time.
- (da) mr president, the lisbon summit will bring its share of fine words. the words are welcome, but the main problem is that we have an economic and monetary union which gives priority to the fight against inflation rather than to employment. the central bank believes that price increases should always be kept below 2%, and that under the stability pact the budget deficit should not exceed 1%. it is never stated that unemployment should be less than 5% or - better still - 2% or even 1%. the price of silver cannot rise; public consumption cannot increase. one might imagine that a country wants to reduce its unemployment rate through public investment, but this is forbidden. it is more important to continue to regard monetary policy as a sacred cow than to provide jobs for the unemployed. i think it is more important to think about the economic situation of the families and to ensure that the father of the family brings home a pay slip rather than a letter of dismissal, and this was also the view of the danish government, which came to power in 1993. in 1992, the unemployment rate in the 11 euroland countries was the same as in denmark, at 9.2%. the unemployment rate in denmark rose to 5.1% from 1992 to 1998, while it rose to 10.9% in euroland. denmark recorded an overall growth of 20%, compared with 10% in the euro area. if the danish government had acted in the same way today, it would have acted in full illegality and contrary to the constitution, since the budget deficit was 3.9% at the time. denmark now also has a budget surplus, because those who received a pay slip instead of a letter of dismissal have started to pay taxes instead of receiving benefits. the prime ministers would do well to study the danish example when they meet in lisbon to discuss the economy. what we need is an amendment to the emu treaty and not some kind of talk to the unemployed. my group hopes that the danish electorate will decide to retain the danish krone when they are called upon to vote in a referendum on emu membership on 28 september this year.
- (da) mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr president of the commission, i should like to express my satisfaction at the offensive nature of the agenda for the lisbon summit, which is aimed at promoting employment and economic development. this agenda emphasises the need for a strong and dynamic economy and increased competitiveness in order to guarantee our objective of increasing employment in the eu. the question is why the eu is so far behind the us in research, in developing new technologies and in creating new jobs. we need to implement joint actions to strengthen research and development, including research in information technology, and we need to strengthen the internal market and lay the foundations for taking full advantage of new technologies and developing trade via the internet. we are right to emphasise the importance of training and the access and use of the internet by public administrations; this is what we have called the "cognitive society". we are also right to emphasize access to risk capital and the need to improve the situation of small and mediumsized enterprises. the commission's presentation to the summit set out quantified objectives for employment development, unemployment and women's employment. it also set a target of reducing poverty-related problems in europe over the next ten years. i think it is right to set common objectives, because the policies of the member states in this area will necessarily be different. some member states of the eu - i will not mention them for fear of forgetting some - are facing a high level of youth unemployment. in others, including my own country, denmark, the rate of youth unemployment is very low; the fact is that employers and training institutions are in a bitter struggle to win the young people's favour. each country has its own problems, its own systems, its own culture and its own traditions. the choice of means to achieve these common objectives of increasing employment and reducing social exclusion must therefore reflect these differences. the employment policy developed in luxembourg, based on an exchange of experience and ideas, guarantees this approach and, despite the many common problems we will face in the coming years, whether in the field of employment policy or social policy, some common challenges can only be solved individually. but it is also vital that states cooperate and inspire each other in trying to find solutions to social problems rather than getting in each other's way. it is important that we set common objectives, but it is also necessary that each country retains sovereignty and responsibility in the formulation of its social and employment policy and that we maintain a certain degree of competition between systems in order to ensure the dynamism and evolution of social and employment policy.

- (da) mr president, the prodi commission will not last five years, because people will not accept that their behaviour is constantly regulated in the smallest detail by brussels. it is difficult to predict who will strike the saving blow and when it will be delivered. this is often the case with historical events. random events happen, insignificant things suddenly become very important, because an insignificant thing can become a symbol of a deep evil. the fundamental contradiction is that the union is proclaiming itself more and more democratic, while democracy is being systematically undermined in our states. the voters will one day exclaim, like the little boy in andersen's fairy tale the emperor's new clothes: "but he has no clothes at all". last month mr prodi spoke in favour of a radical decentralisation of the union's activities, whereas the five-year programme envisages radical centralisation with the transfer of new legislative matters - and many new matters - from the national and regional parliaments of the member states to the prodi commission in brussels and the adoption by 62 votes out of 87 in the council. it's like being in an orwell novel. the so-called 'decentralisation' is in fact a centralisation. the name 'democracy' is actually a rejection of democracy. under the heading 'more transparency', the commission is proposing a system which aims to ban access to documents which are now accessible. when he was sworn in, mr prodi solemnly promised our group and the house that he would adopt a completely innovative approach to transparency. any document provided by the commission to others would also be provided to us. the elected representatives of the people would no longer be the last to know. we would no longer have to wait behind the windows of the permanent exhibitions and watch the students consult documents that we cannot obtain. the days are over when european employers or the committee of agricultural trade unions could consult documents which we could not obtain. a fundamental rule of democracy is to ensure that the executive serves the electors and the elected representatives of the people. within the eu, legislative power is transferred to the executive. the elected representatives of the people are granted privileges, but the basic documents, which emanate from the decision-making power, are kept secret. we decided at the conference of presidents to call the ombudsman and mr prodi to explain the concept of transparency. i hope mr prodi will say: 'i'm sorry, but i didn't know that my services were doing the opposite of what i had promised. i 'm true to my word . here's my signature for the transparency i promised". i thank you, mr president, although i should like to say thank you.
- (da) mr president, i have good reason to believe that we will reach a good compromise on the directive on cocoa and chocolate products, thanks in particular to the efforts of mr paul lannoye. i agree with the desire to make labelling clear, that is to say, to give consumers good information. since a number of countries have hitherto known only chocolate in which the vegetable fat used for its manufacture is cocoa butter, it may be of interest to consumers in those countries to know that another vegetable fat has been used. i do not think, however, that it can or should be assumed that the quality of chocolate produced from shea nuts is inferior to that of chocolate produced from cocoa butter. this is not based on any objective criteria and the council is right to leave it to the consumers to assess quality for themselves. let us not forget that shea nuts are as vital to the countries that export them as cocoa is to the countries that export cocoa. therefore, a future study to measure the impact of this directive on exports from developing countries should also include cocoa and shea nuts. the main thing for me is to ensure that the vegetable fat in chocolate cannot be altered by enzymatic or genetic engineering. such a ban is the only way to ensure that developing countries can continue to export foodstuffs produced naturally.
- (da) the conventions obviously concern relations between states and citizens; however, since the eu is not a state, it is not right that it should increasingly behave as if it were one and that it should acquire powers comparable to those of a state. there is therefore a gap in fundamental rights and this gap must obviously be closed. however, our zeal for doing what is right can cause serious harm. the eu must choose: should we guarantee our own rights, should we close in on ourselves or should we strengthen human rights across europe? the eu cannot compete with existing bodies responsible for settling disputes, such as the court of human rights in strasbourg. this would weaken it to the detriment of human rights throughout europe. i therefore believe that the european court should be kept out of the fundamental rights project. the eu should instead take the strasbourg judgments seriously and require current and future member states to ratify the european convention on human rights, the ilo conventions and the main un conventions and make economic and customs agreements conditional on the respect of these conventions by the partners concerned. this is how the eu will strengthen compliance with the existing conventions and this is how it should be.
commission - mr rübig, i shall concentrate my answer on the vat question. you asked me about the commission's work programme and i said, as a precaution, that we had not raised this issue today, that the commission had its work programme and that my colleagues directly concerned would come to speak to you about it at the appropriate time. however, you ask me, in general terms, whether the great project of modernising vat could be adopted with your proposals by qualified majority? i would say that it depends on what is meant by the 'major project to modernise vat'. in particular, if you are thinking of the transition from the transitional regime to the final vat system, which would change the place of taxation, we have made it clear, and i confirm it, that this point would be decided unanimously anyway. as regards the modernisation of the regime in the face of new economic developments - i am thinking in particular of all the consequences of globalisation, liberalisation, electronic commerce - that is a question you can put to mr bolkenstein. we are working with the member states to identify the elements of this package. a meeting was held on 2 march and i think very sincerely that it is a little too early to give you any further details. but i have spoken to you about the modernisation of vat, which makes it possible to make the transition and say to mr helmer: i am well aware that these are delicate issues, i know the importance attached, in the name of national sovereignty, to taxation, to voting on taxes and, in particular, to voting on rates: i have sat for twenty-two years in parliament in my own country. i simply wish to use all my powers of persuasion with you, of course, and if possible with the negotiators and mr tsatsos and mr brok, to say to the states which are reluctant and more susceptible than others on this subject: 'be very careful before you refuse the extension of the qualified majority on proposals which the commission has made in a reasonable manner. "we are talking about the proper functioning of the internal market, we are talking about the completion of this internal market and all the countries are committed to it, because all the countries of the union have an interest in it. but what will happen in a very enlarged europe, where disparities will be even greater than they are today in social terms and in terms of budgets? that is why we recommend an extremely lucid, i was going to say almost clinical, examination of these questions in the interests of the internal market. finally, i thank professor tsatsos for having pointed out the link between these proposals and the role of the european parliament. since i have the opportunity to do so, i confirm that one of the commission's proposals is to associate directly codecision with all measures which would be taken by qualified majority as soon as they are legislative measures. it is clear from these subjects which concern the internal market - social security or taxation - and its proper functioning that there is a legitimate place for the european parliament to express its views and take a position. we shall have further opportunities to say so and to try to convince the other negotiators of this in the months ahead.

- (da) mr president, allow me first of all to congratulate mr blokland on his report. we know that waste incineration is a very complex and technical area, but i think this report is a worthy piece of work. unlike mrs grossetête, i believe that the common position and the blokland report will bring about a significant improvement in the environmental quality of the incineration of waste, whether hazardous or not. i believe that we will have a useful tool to prevent and limit the damage that waste incineration does to the environment and we will also have a useful tool to prevent and limit the health risks we as human beings face. the report shall also provide for the use of appropriate means. i will not hesitate to support the point that there must be real waste planning with waste sorting so that waste incineration is linked to the whole of community waste legislation and the problem of incineration is high up in the waste hierarchy. i also agree with the obligation to indicate very precisely the installations covered by these rules and the types of waste which benefit from a derogation. as regards the definition of co-incineration plants, in particular cement plants - and this is a complex issue which has been the subject of very aggressive lobbying in the context of the examination of this case - i would say that in my view we can support amendment no 43, which provides useful clarifications to the common position. finally, there is the fundamental question of setting emission limit values for air and water - whether for heavy metals or dust, ammonia, dioxins, sulphur dioxide or other dangerous substances. the levels found are, in my view, satisfactory and i believe that the balance between single incineration plants and co-incineration plants is good. i hope that this balance will be maintained following the vote in plenary tomorrow. and again, unlike mrs grossetête, i will say that the emission levels are, in my view, realistic and technically feasible, especially since they are applied in a number of member states. in addition, existing plants which do not currently meet the requirements are given a very long period of time to adapt to the new standards. i hope that by voting tomorrow we will be able to make our contribution to finding a solution to the environmental and health problems posed by waste incineration, so that the enormous volumes of waste we produce every day do not cause more environmental damage or health problems than is strictly necessary.
- (da) mr president, let me first of all thank mr chris davies for this report. the adoption of the commission proposal and the davies report will bring a significant improvement in air quality for european citizens. there are, in my view, two decisive points. first, we must adhere to the guidelines set out by the who. i believe that this will be an important step forward in the prevention of serious illness and premature death, especially among the most vulnerable groups of the population, especially children. the second point which i consider very important is to set certain dates for which the provisional values must have been reached by 2010, but also dates for the long-term objectives by 2020. i think it is essential that a date be fixed in order to be able to achieve the objectives set. i would also like to make a few comments on the amendments proposed by the committee on industry which seek to reduce the scope of the commission's initiative by allowing far too long a transition from the who recommendations and by significantly delaying efforts to achieve the who values. these amendments do not merely devalue the commission's proposal; they also represent a deterioration and a step backwards in relation to the situation we are currently experiencing in europe. as mr davies has pointed out, the level is worse than in the united states. i do not understand how mr langen can call this, as he did a moment ago, a compromise or a compromise. these amendments are, in my view, totally incomprehensible and irresponsible. they will have serious consequences for health and the environment in europe and will result in a huge multi-billion dollar bill for european health services. i therefore warmly recommend support for the commission's proposal and the davies report.
the rapporteur. - (da) mr president, talking about human rights is not talking into the air. nor is it a point on the agendas of the various parties. these rights are universal; in other words, they are the same across borders and regardless of party. they are not opinions, but actions. they protect individuals against injustice and discrimination. they shall be clear and easy to understand; it can't be a bunch of bullshit. human rights must be based on a solid foundation; the european parliament has always been a party to the convention on the rights of the child, and this is why we must rely above all on the conventions concluded by the states and on the case law so brilliantly established by the european court of human rights since the second world war. the eu must not alter or rebuild this base. the eu must, on the contrary, enjoy the status of a legal person and adhere to human rights conventions and thus guarantee respect for human rights in its sphere of decision-making as well. the case law i have mentioned is not static. fortunately, it is in constant evolution. in this connection, recall the recent ruling on the equality of rights of homosexuals, which led britain to change its legislation, and i hope that the same will soon be done in austria. i also hope that we will soon have a judgment guaranteeing that citizens not only have the right to form trade unions, but also the right not to have to join them. new clauses are also being added to existing conventions. we have conventions on bioethics, on the protection of personal data and on the protection of privacy, but i would like to stress today two new conventions on national minorities, one from 1995 and the other from 1998. i think that bosnia and kosovo have taught us that national minorities are the main powder keg for the europe of tomorrow. we must demand of the new member states and of turkey that if they wish to be part of the community they must guarantee the right of their national minorities to live in peace and to speak the language which they have always spoken in their region. it is not enough for new countries to comply with these provisions today; the european union is also committed to the principle of subsidiarity, and will have to guarantee that it will do so in the future, and will therefore have to sign and ratify such conventions if it has not already done so. our present member states will also have to do so. we must therefore urge those countries which have not yet ratified these conventions on national minorities to do so. two member states - belgium and france - have still not signed the convention on the protection of national minorities after five years, while these two countries are clearly keen to look at the situation in other countries. let them first put things in order in their own countries and sign these conventions! the standards we set for others must also be set for ourselves. we must demand that the 15 governments sign and ratify the conventions. we must remind them of the regional and minority languages pact and the convention on the international tribunal for war criminals, which only italy has ratified. we must all ratify this convention, because the only way to avoid the vicious circle of revenge and self-justice is to bring war criminals to justice and to put them on trial. i will also point out the convention on torture which ireland has not ratified; the commission has also been asked to provide information on the implementation of the un convention against torture. i could go on for a long time, particularly on the conditions of asylum seekers in our member states. if half of what amnesty international and human rights watch report is true, it must be admitted that the situation of asylum seekers is unsustainable. i could also speak about the rights of the child - a huge problem, particularly in a number of candidate countries. i could mention the discrimination which still affects disabled people and, in continuation of what mrs cecilia malmström said, i would like to stress that the problem of equal rights for men and women is still not solved in the member states of the union. finally, i would like to say that we may need new instruments to monitor respect for human rights and, as far as national minorities are concerned, it might be interesting if, within the framework of the vienna observatory on racism, we had a monitoring body for the treatment of national minorities. i would also add my proposal to create a supervisory body to protect personal data and privacy. technology is changing and our instruments for monitoring human rights must also change. (applause) i would like to thank
- (da) i do not wish to spoil the good atmosphere in this house, because for 25 years i have been studying and analysing the human rights situation and i have seen that good intentions mixed with a total ignorance of reality produce terrible results - as we say in the spoken language: 'everyone talks about rain and good weather, but nothing can be done about it'. it 's not so much what we say that matters; i would even say that the more we talk about human rights, the worse the violations. let me make three brief remarks. firstly, i fully share the view expressed by my fellow countryman and rapporteur, mr haarder, that we must impose on ourselves the same requirements for the protection of minorities as we impose on the applicant countries. the european institutions should have thought of this in 1991-92, when they recognized slovenia first and croatia second, contrary to the rules in force on the protection of minorities, and thus added fuel to the fire and triggered the balkan catastrophe. the second point i would like to make is the continuation of yesterday's debate. the fact that we would absolutely want to inscribe the charter of human rights in a european constitution - in the eu treaty - would not only lead to colossal and insurmountable constitutional problems, especially in a country like denmark, but the eu treaty would become a real constitution with fundamental rights. it could be said that the more rights we have, the better off we are, but rights are part of a complex systemic whole. the adoption of a european charter would lead us into a complex and endless legal and constitutional quagmire. what will be the decision-making body? does the convention on human rights guarantee rights better than the european charter? is it better than national constitutions? it is absurd to ask such questions. finally - and this is my third point - i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr haarder. i note with satisfaction and great interest that paragraph 61 highlights the collective right of trade unions. however, mr haarder's old battle horse, the right of individuals not to be required to join a union, was not included as a recommendation. i congratulate mr haarder on the new political vision expressed in the report.
the rapporteur. - (da) mr president, i should like to thank the house for its broad support for the main lines of this report. human rights concern us all, across party and national boundaries; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i would like to thank mrs swiebel in particular for her kind words to me today and for the close cooperation that has been established between us, which has obviously allowed her to exert a considerable influence. i would like to tell her that i will be happy to vote on the additional amendments she has tabled and which we will be voting on tomorrow. i would also like to thank mr pirker and mrs palacio. i thank you for the broad consensus on this report. i welcome this, especially in this year in which the european union has decided to give high priority to the human rights situation. finally, i will tell mr krarup that there has been an error in paragraph 61 - ten words have not been included in the danish version. mr krarup should therefore be able to suppress the joy he had expressed about a possible change. reading the english version will help him to know the exact meaning of this point.
in writing. - (da) competition is the basis of the well-being enjoyed by eu citizens. and the adoption of a new directive on chocolate products is precisely aimed at improving this competition within the eu. we must be very careful about food policy and consumer protection. however, this is not about health or environmental protection. it is a question of determining to what extent we should allow free competition - as long as it does not lead to any health risks - in the chocolate sector so that all chocolate producers can sell their products in all eu countries and not just in the seven countries which currently allow the vegetable fats in question. for consumers, this is purely a matter of taste. it is therefore unacceptable for politicians to take the place of consumers in determining their choices. the products must be available, their contents must be clearly indicated, but we must trust the consumers' ability to discern. it can't be otherwise. it is up to the consumers to decide what tastes best! the strong opposition to the proposal which we can now see in this house can only be due to an attempt by the chocolate producers to curb competition. when we look at the origin of the opposition movements, it is clear that, beyond the groups, this opposition comes primarily from countries that take advantage of the rules in force to protect their chocolate industry.
- (da) madam president, allow me to thank mr haarder for the very good work he has done. there is one point, however, on which i do not agree with the rapporteur. when i read the danish version of the haarder report, i thought for a moment that mr haarder had acquired wisdom over the years and i had noted that the danish supreme court had ruled that so-called exclusivity agreements were not contrary to human rights. i believed and hoped that i could join mr haarder in stressing the right of everyone to join trade union organizations. however, when the report is read in the other languages, it is clear that mr haarder has not acquired more wisdom or is hiding it well. some vague wording suggests that the rapporteur is continuing his crusade against the danish model, which allows workers and employers to decide that only members of a specific union can be employed by a well-defined company. this system does not deprive anyone of the right to work, it does not deprive anyone of the right to join a trade union, but it cannot be accepted that people benefit from extended social agreements if they are not themselves members of the trade union which is the basis of these agreements. mr haarder's attack goes much further, since he is indirectly trying to strengthen the trade unions which do not recognise the collective measures which have ultimately helped to establish the welfare and human rights we have been discussing since this morning.
counsel. - (pt) madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i do not intend to speak for long. i think that the debate we are having here today is of the utmost importance and that the different sensitivities that are expressed in the readings of the various reports raise interesting questions, some of which are quite controversial and others highlight contradictory aspects. some people agree with the form, but probably not with the content. in any case, i think these are four reports which, taken together, raise issues of the highest importance and of great relevance to us all, especially at a time when the european union, both internally and in its external affirmation, is trying to give substance and to emphasize a set of basic values which are basically the leaven which can serve as the basis for its own political union. we believe that it is impossible to guarantee the deepening of the european union if we are not prepared to guarantee, also among ourselves, a common set of principles and to guarantee that these principles are also the essential basis of our own external projection. i said exterior projection and i said interior dimension. there has been a lot of talk today, directly and indirectly, about the attitude towards austria; it was done in a relatively emotional way, i would say even naturally emotional, by people connected to this country. the portuguese presidency, the portuguese prime minister, having explained the position expressed by the 14 countries of the european union in their bilateral relations with austria, i would like it to be very clear that we do not take any negative view of austria's behaviour at international level and, in particular, of austria's respect for its duties and obligations within the framework of the european union. this is an undeniable fact: austria has been a country which respects the european union. but one thing must be known, and it is irrefutable in our interpretation of the european situation: we are all austrian citizens. today, in the european union, citizenship is european citizenship. the austrian citizen is a portuguese citizen. a portuguese citizen is an austrian citizen. there is no question of an internal nature when given ideologies and given political forces propagate and defend a certain type of ideology, even if later, for tactical reasons, they take a step back from these positions. we cannot consider that other countries are indifferent to this. we are working today in a european space, a common space which is intended to be limited to a basic set of principles, such as many of those highlighted in the reports under consideration. we must have a european perspective on these issues and we cannot systematically take refuge behind the alibi of subsidiarity to say that these are national issues and that this is interference in the internal affairs of a given country. that's not true, man. as i said, austria's internal affairs are the internal affairs of the european union. we do not believe that by advocating the need for austrian political life to respect the foundations and principles of the european union, which austria has committed itself to defending at the level of the european union, we are interfering in the internal affairs of that country. apart from the emotional questions and, of course, the reflexes that this type of situation can engender, we believe that we must give serious thought to what this act means, an act which has a bilateral dimension - it must be said - an act, let's be clear, of political prophylaxis. we believe that political prevention is sometimes the best way to prevent future diseases and, in particular, the resurgence of old ghosts at european level. in the field of external relations, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, we believe that the european union already has a solid framework of values which will only be guaranteed and enhanced by deepening it, particularly through these reports. there are, of course, various controversial issues in this area. different fields have differentiated readings. when we talk about racism and we are all against racism, which is a unanimous statement in this house, i do not know whether we are all really in agreement, whether we are all in favour of concrete measures to combat racism. when we all say that we are against xenophobia, i do not know whether we are all capable of accepting active measures to integrate foreign nationals within the european union and to accept measures to protect the interests of foreigners in the union. we're going to have an interesting test. the test is the charter of fundamental rights of the citizens of the union. if we had the will within the union to extend this charter to foreign nationals living within the union, we would be sending a signal that our action against xenophobia starts within our own home and begins with measures that we ourselves decide to implement at the level of domestic policy. i would also like to say that, as regards the fight against discrimination and the various forms of discrimination covered by the various reports, there is of course a range of differentiated measures. we know that even in the domestic legislation of our states there are great differences in the way some of these questions are dealt with. but i also think that the way that these questions are asked here, the way that these reports help us think about these issues, is a boost to the collective consciousness and an important element in being able to continue to deepen the debate on these kinds of issues. this debate is not just the responsibility of governments, but also of non-governmental organizations, social lobbies, political lobbies. this question must remain on the agenda, and that is why this debate is so important. finally, and to conclude, i would like to draw attention to all the measures which the council of the european union has already taken in this field, to the various joint actions which have been carried out. among other things, a set of initiatives related to the european year against racism in 1997. i would like to stress one very important aspect which we have probably not fully grasped: what the new amsterdam treaty represents in this respect. it was not easy to reach consensus at the amsterdam negotiations, and i participated in those negotiations, it was not easy to reach consensus on the integration of all the measures which now make up articles 6 and 7. we will probably have to improve, now that we have gained experience in this area, what already exists, improve this joint. we shall see, in the framework of the intergovernmental conference, whether it is possible to reach a consensus which will enable us to make progress in this area. but i would also draw attention to the importance of an event - which will be symbolically held in vienna on 8 april - namely the launching of the european observatory on racism and xenophobia. this structure will be very important and i think it will also be important to ensure that everyone works together to promote an effective fight against racism and xenophobia within the european union, in what is, in essence, the development of what we can call the 'ethical pillar' of our union. it is a union of principles, a union which we must deepen, and there will be no political union in the future if we fail to ensure that these principles are shared by the vast majority of the community.
commission - madam president, at the end of this extremely interesting debate, i might perhaps explain the division of tasks or the division of labour within the commission. i shall concentrate on the malmström and belder reports and my colleague, commissioner vitorino, will deal with the other two important reports - we are of course in danger of repeating ourselves from time to time. let me start with the malmström report. i think that the first annual report of this new european parliament is profound and timely. i hope that before the summer break this new commission will adopt a communication on human rights in foreign policy. it will cover many areas of the report and all the recommendations of parliament will be a useful contribution to our work. i cannot at this stage answer all the points in the report. i shall rather follow up on this, as well as on the discussions with the european parliament, in the coming months, in order to produce a good communication, which will reflect the important arguments of this report. i should like to focus my first remarks on the rights of women and children, as the rapporteur has chosen to emphasize this important point. this is a timely choice in view of the beijing +5 platform for action which will meet in new york this year at the united nations general assembly. beijing confirmed that women's rights are universal human rights and that women have a central role to play in building democratic and just societies. the european initiative for democracy and human rights is a reflection of this. women are explicitly included as a target group to be supported. the actions cover a wide range of concerns, from women's participation in decision-making in the balkans to support for domestic women in côte d'ivoire. problems such as violence against women have rightly been defined as human rights problems. as a result, they should be vigorously promoted as an integral part of our foreign policy and development aid. the report by mrs malmström sets out some of the harsh realities of violence against women in many forms and at all levels of society. from the horror of rape to sexual mutilation, from the home to prison. our daphne and stop programmes are the backbone of the commission's response. the daphne programme has supported information sharing and good practice within ngos and member states through over 100 transnational projects. a new daphne programme was adopted in december 1999 and will be allocated €5 million per year for the period 2000-03. following the european parliament resolution of 1997, the commission has been conducting an awareness-raising campaign on domestic violence in the member states. we should use what we learn from these campaigns and share this knowledge with the candidate countries, perhaps in the framework of the daphne programme. i take note of what the report says about this horrible practice of female genital mutilation and the lack of data on this subject. the commission will try to find a way to help in the search for a solution. the commission is also working with others to stop the abominable trafficking of women which has increased in recent years. we published a communication on this in 1998. we then focused on prevention, research, law enforcement, effective prosecution of those responsible for trafficking in women and assistance to victims. we are working on this, as the honourable member knows, with ngos, the osce and the candidate countries. in the field of children's rights, the stop programme has co-financed 67 projects on trafficking and sexual exploitation of children, with eur 6.5 million for the first five years. a new stop programme is planned to be launched this year, which will be open to the applicant countries. i shall now turn to more general points. i mentioned the publication of a communication on human rights and external relations. my aim is to adopt a more effective and coherent policy and spending approach, in particular to improve the management of our programmes in this area. human rights projects are very different from projects to build bridges or to strengthen primary education. ngos and civil society partners are integral to their implementation. in the field, they are often conducted in delicate or dangerous conditions. those who implement projects may even find their own rights threatened, which is no doubt why ngos are often saddened to have to return a stack of receipts to the commission. the financial procedures of the european union, including the financial regulations, are not favourable to ngos - to say the least - which leads us to the question: should we stop funding ngos or should we change our procedures? the first proposal is both impossible and undesirable. we cannot do without ngos. reform of our procedures, many of which were defined by the member states at a time when ngos were barely in existence, will take time, but we are working on it, among other things, as part of the overall reform package. unfortunately, change will not come overnight. i come now to the budget and parliament's proposal for a joint declaration on the annual increase in human rights expenditure. we cannot separate the size of the budget from the question of our management capacity. otherwise we will make too many promises which we simply cannot keep, and when it comes to supporting ngos, we often go too far. unfulfilled promises can quickly lead to ruin. we have a number of projects to complete and we have not spent all the resources available in the human rights budget in the past, so i think it would be very difficult to justify an increase in expenditure without explaining this. but i want to make it clear that i want to ensure that we can spend all the resources as soon as possible and thereby justify a substantial increase in expenditure. the possibility of setting up a human rights agency, as envisaged in vienna and cologne, could help us. i think this agency should be operational and not consultative. i'll think about it in the communication. the possibility of creating an agency should also be considered as part of a wider reform of our external aid and of outsourcing as a whole. i now turn to the points raised in the belder report, which concerns the commission's communication on combating racism, xenophobia and anti-semitism in the applicant countries. i will answer on behalf of myself and my colleague, commissioner verheugen. i would like to congratulate the authors of this detailed report. the commission is of course critical of the lack of specific proposals in the commission's communication, but i would point out that the adoption of this communication took place after the resignation of the college in march 1999, when the commission, and i will say this with a mild tone, was not in the best position to formulate new proposals. i hope that this answer will serve as a guide for the future. the fight against racism is an essential element of the union's human rights policies and the preaccession strategy. it consists of two main elements: the adoption and implementation by the candidate countries of the acquis communautaire, including the 1996 joint action on racism and xenophobia, the 1951 geneva convention and the 1967 new york protocol on asylum seekers and refugees, and the proper use of our cooperation programmes. the compatibility of minority legislation is addressed during the screening process and during bilateral and association committee discussions. we have examined the language laws in lithuania, estonia and slovakia, a point raised during the debates, and the legislation on roma in the czech republic and slovakia. these discussions have produced tangible results. the second element, our cooperation programmes, comprises the national programmes phare, phare-democracy and the meda programme. the phare programme attaches great importance to the reform of the police, criminal justice and asylum systems, as the rapporteurs propose. this reform will help to bring about the necessary attitude changes within the institutions. the objective of raising police awareness of racism and xenophobia has also been integrated into the european programmes for the education and training of police forces in central and eastern europe. in these matters we will be persevering. we are also willing to strengthen them. a central objective, which is reflected in both the malmström report and the belder report, is to strengthen our relations with ngos in the candidate countries. the new access programme, replacing the linen and partnership programmes, is designed to strengthen civil society. finally, with regard to the roma, whose conditions were discussed by several speakers, the commission is trying to be more consistent in its support for roma communities. we intend to be associated with the meeting to be organised by the european observatory to which members of the european parliament will be invited - a meeting organised to stimulate ideas for improving the european union's assistance to roma communities. these reports cover extremely important subjects. these are issues of growing concern to the citizens of the european union, who are daily bombarded by the media with news of human rights violations, whether in the caucasus, colombia, the balkans, sierra leone, china, etc. i very much hope that the european union will raise these violations at the un commission on human rights in geneva, where i will be on 27 march. i thank parliament for its valuable contribution. the commission and parliament are well placed to work together on the issue of human rights. we are well placed to lead the way in this area. i am particularly pleased to be working with members of this house to ensure that the communication we will be publishing later this year is intellectually and politically exemplary and can be seen by ngos, parliamentarians and others as a beacon in the debate on these vital issues.
the rapporteur. - (da) madam president, if i understand my friend mr seixas da costa correctly, he is defending the sanctions against austria. i would therefore ask him, as a matter of conscience, whether he has tabled this proposal as president-in-office of the council, that is to say, on behalf of the council, or on behalf of the 14 governments.
- (da) mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we have spent an entire day in the european parliament discussing human rights and talking about high ideals. i therefore find it somewhat shameful that the situation should be totally different from the moment the union is called upon to take concrete action. i believe that the commission and the council are going too far in their desire to control asylum seekers and third country nationals entering the fortress europe by creating eurodac. the eurodac initiative has a number of absurdities and infringements. firstly, the decision to retain fingerprints of minors - already from the age of 14 - who have not committed any criminal offence is contrary to the united nations convention on the rights of the child, which calls on us to protect children, to respect the principle that a child is not an adult until the age of 18 unless another age has been legally fixed by a state, which i believe is not the case for the european union. the decision to retain fingerprints for a period of ten years, even if the person concerned has not committed any illegal act and even if he may have obtained a residence permit within the union, constitutes a violation of the european convention on human rights, which requires respect for privacy. the amalgamation of asylum seekers and illegal immigrants is a violation of the refugee convention. the human and fundamental rights of which so much is said are worthless if we are not able to guarantee them in practice, especially for those who manage to infiltrate the fortress europe. the impression that people have or bring home from our country of an 'area of freedom, security and justice' does not reflect the values that we so pompously call international and universal. i therefore ask the commission and the council to let eurodac die a good death. that's all she deserves.
- (da) mr president, on behalf of the europe of nations group, i would like to express my confidence in the way the council has dealt with this matter and, like other members, i would ask the council to vote in favour of the establishment of eurodac. we are talking about creating an area for public safety and i find it astonishing that anyone can express any suspicion of the proper administration of this regulation. i feel that this regulation is intended to guarantee the rights of legitimate asylum seekers and there is no question of infringing the rights of legitimate and honest asylum seekers. i cannot imagine that the council or the commission would wish to create a system which would restrict the freedom of law-abiding citizens. the establishment of eurodac is in line with the creation of an area of freedom and justice which we support.
counsel. - (pt) it is obvious, mr president, that the union does not have to intervene in the constitutional organisation of the member states, just as it is not the union's task to define a so-called positive list of parties for which it is appropriate to vote. if we venture into this area, we would be operating in minefields. this does not mean that the underlying issue is not politically significant: as is the case in various countries, in my country for example, a nazi or fascist party has no right to register with a constitutional court. it is therefore not acceptable as a legitimate party in democratic life. other countries have different systems and they are just as respectable as the one in my country. when we enter an enlarged community area, we must understand that no situation in one country with regard to democratic rights is indifferent to other countries and their nationals. we are all citizens of europe and we are all citizens of a europe politically governed by a set of basic principles. if these principles are not respected, we have the right and the duty to sound the alarm. it is to be hoped that there will never be a list of desirable and undesirable parties in the european union.
- yes, but that's exactly what it is. because the governor of an austrian province, more precisely the one whose capital is klagenfurt, has made statements that i hate as much as you do, you are punishing a country and you are punishing a people, including people who were able to vote against the party in question, without specifying what the crime is. so far, you have sanctioned opinions, not actions. if these were acts, denmark might have to be punished for pursuing an anti-immigration policy that the fpö has not yet pursued. so you sanctioned opinions, not deeds. i would therefore like to know, when i vote next time, whether portugal and other countries - outside the council - will propose sanctions against my country if, for example, the present government decides to admit ministers from the socialist people's party or the unity list, who are against economic and monetary union, or whether these countries will oppose the appointment of a government including the liberal mr fogh rasmussen if it is based on the danish people's party, which many newspapers describe as the danish equivalent of the austrian fpö. it would be helpful if people knew that. but perhaps you regret the action of the 14 prime ministers?

the rapporteur. - (da) i confirm that mr imbeni's interpretation is correct. the italian, danish and english versions are correct, that is to say, as mr imbeni has pointed out, accurate.
the rapporteur. - (da) mr president, a number of words have not been included in the danish version in paragraph 61. the english version is the correct one. in the danish version, after the word "sammenslutninger" (trade unions) add: "og den enkeltes rettigheder i forhold til faglige sammenslutninger" (and the rights of individuals with regard to trade unions). it is not only the right to form trade unions, but also the rights of each individual in relation to these trade unions.
in writing. - (da) in view of the gross violations of human rights in mexico, we cannot vote on the report. this was recently shown in a december 1999 report by amnesty international, which highlighted the serious problems in the chiapas region. the trade agreement must include a clause demanding the development of democracy in mexico - including respect for human rights - but if this demand is not followed by concrete effects, it will be mere words. the previous trade agreement with mexico already contained a clause calling for more democracy, but this was never respected. the international organizations such as the united nations and the individual countries must therefore put pressure on mexico and only when there are significant improvements can a new trade agreement be concluded with mexico, in our view.
in writing. - (da) the european parliament's report on the charter is characterized by one fundamental element: whether one likes it or not, the desire to establish european citizenship in a supranational state. this is a fundamental difference from our conception of a european community. the union is and must remain an association of states. we must therefore ensure that the charter has no impact on the national constitutions or other comparable regulations of the member states on fundamental rights. this does not mean that we support the idea that those in power can run a country as they please; we simply consider that human rights are universal and must be guaranteed by universal instruments. on the other hand, we do not want to see two sets of rights created on the european continent - one set of rights for eu citizens and another for citizens of european third countries. it is therefore essential that a possible eu charter simply requires eu member states to ratify existing international conventions and declarations on rights without going further. the granting of an increasing number of competences to the eu as a state, without being obliged, for example, to respect the european convention on human rights, is posing increasing problems. however, we do not believe that the eu's accession to the european convention on human rights as a legal person is a solution, because we do not share the idea that the eu has or should have a special political status. we also do not approve of the constitutional process of which the adoption of a charter is an integral part. we prefer that the treaty should specify the obligation for the eu authorities to respect the jurisdiction and decisions of the court of human rights. such a practice would make it possible to condemn a questionable aspect of community law when a member state makes use of that law. the jurisdiction of the european court of justice cannot cover areas already guaranteed by the strasbourg court of human rights or other international bodies. if the existing conventions are considered insufficient, for example because biotechnological development creates new problems, the issue becomes international and solutions must be found at international level. this is not an eu-only issue and we must seek international/global consensus in international fora. that is why we shall vote against the whole report.
in writing. - (da) the four danish liberal members, bertel haarder, anne e. jensen, ole andreasen and niels busk, voted today against paragraph 14a of the report, because we do not want the charter to be part of the work of the intergovernmental conference.
in writing. - (da) the european union is founded on the fundamental principles of freedom, democracy and respect for human rights. i personally deplore the desire to introduce a new set of rights in the european treaties, because this is a signal that could cause confusion and i fear that it will endanger the instruments we currently have and which are working well. i think a statement would be more appropriate. the european convention on human rights is an excellent instrument for the protection of human rights in europe. it includes the defence of these rights on the basis of judgments of the european court of human rights, which has acquired extensive jurisdiction and extensive case law in this field. it also has the advantage of not being limited to the eu and of being operational in relation to almost the whole of the european continent. the european court of justice also relies on the fundamental rights of the member states. the desire to give the eu its own set of fundamental and human rights could influence these principles.
in writing. - (da) we agree with the general points in the report which recommend that the member states respect international conventions on human rights. we also share the concerns expressed in the report about the situation of the rights of asylum seekers, minorities, women, children, etc. in certain countries. we are opposed to the idea that the annual report on human rights can be used to speed up integration into the eu. we disagree with the innovative ideas proposed in the report on institutional matters, in particular the idea that the eu should establish a common legal framework and a common policy on refugees and asylum seekers. we believe that human rights problems must be solved at local level and through the existing international institutions and bodies which have been set up to ensure respect for human rights, such as the united nations and the council of europe. we also disapprove of the idea of drawing up a new charter of fundamental rights within the eu, which would be legally binding, because we believe that such a charter would create conflicts with the european convention on human rights. a charter of fundamental rights of the european union would not, by itself, contribute to an improvement of the situation, either at european or international level. despite the above-mentioned reservations, we have nevertheless decided to vote in favour of the report, because we believe that it is extremely important to strive for an improvement in the human rights situation in europe.
in writing. - (da) we approve of the humanist angle from which the report has been drawn up and we approve of the fact that the report emphasizes the reasons for the development of racism and xenophobia in europe. we believe it is important to combat racism and xenophobia by guaranteeing citizens the right to education, the right to work, etc. it is also useful that the report recommends the need to offer third country nationals better integration opportunities. it is for the reasons mentioned above that we have decided to vote for the report, because we believe, in general terms, that racism and xenophobia must absolutely be fought. we would, however, like to stress that we cannot endorse the means advocated in the report to combat racism and xenophobia. initiatives such as the development of a common policy for asylum seekers and eurodac, which imply that asylum seekers should be treated as suspects, will not, in our view, contribute to the fight against racism and xenophobia.
- (da) mr president, i believe that the greatest possible priority should be given to transparency and to informing the european citizens about the activities of the eu. it is very important to inform citizens objectively and honestly about european cooperation in these years, when we are facing such vast challenges as enlargement to central and eastern europe. it is particularly important to explain what the common agricultural policy is, what its objectives are, what its means are and what development opportunities it offers. many categories of people need to be informed; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. these information measures will only bear fruit if we establish a partnership with the agricultural organizations and with the consumers. the common agricultural policy is the first and main integrated policy of the community and it represents a very large part of the community budget. it is therefore also appropriate to clarify that aid schemes guarantee consumers food at prices lower than those they would pay in the absence of subsidies. in other words, all european citizens benefit from aid schemes. the common agricultural policy is constantly evolving and will continue to evolve. it must be better known and better coordinated, both within and between the member states. each country has different conditions; that is why the agricultural organisations are the main actors, because these organisations put farmers first and foremost.
- (da) we support the extension of the proposal, mr president, but i would also like to remind you that agenda 2000, which was agreed last year in berlin, is based on a desire to move towards trade liberalization and to try to reduce current aid schemes and to conclude agreements within the wto framework which will allow, with the help of market mechanisms, to increase access to many international markets. let me also remind you that traditional agriculture has been under pressure in recent years. in general, prices for all products fell. some call this stability, but farmers see it differently, as costs rise; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the price system is not affected by the price increases. i would also remind you that the commission recently decided to strengthen the conditions for intervention in the cereals sector. it is mainly the wheat producers who will be hit very hard - and they are already hit by a 15% fall in prices. this will be accompanied by a tightening of quality and the concept of protein, which will lead - especially in the northern part of the eu - to further price cuts. my group - i repeat - is in favour of extending the regulation, but i urge the commission to seriously examine, before 2002-03, the possibilities for changing the way we use the available funds so that we can continue to offer guarantees to this category of producers, who have so far been protected by these aid schemes.
