- madam president, in your comments on the danish rejection of the euro, mrs fontaine, you said that the nazis were appealing to an irrational fear. if you had been better informed, you would know that your description only fits - and only approximately - about 20% of danish naysayers. for the vast majority of those who voted no, the vote was about democratic legitimacy to make decisions about our economic policy. the aim was to avoid important decisions being taken on the same uninformed basis as that on which you are unfortunately speaking. the danish people want a much clearer definition of what the eu should be dealing with and what the national parliaments should continue to decide. but in december in nice, majority voting will be introduced in the social and labour market fields in articles 42 and 137. if, subsequently, article 42 is adopted as the basis for an extension of the circle of persons in regulation 1408, and if, on the basis of article 137, majority decisions are introduced on, for example, the criteria for the granting of unemployment benefits, this will have major consequences for both social and labour market policy in denmark. we are the only eu country which has tax financing of its social benefits and we regulate our labour market by collective agreements. it is simply the basis of the way we have set ourselves up. and the rejection of the euro is, among other things, an expression of our wish to retain the decision-making powers of the danish parliament in these areas, and we also want to do so after nice.
- madam president, after hearing my danish colleague, you can see the stupid level of debate in denmark, a debate which led to a 'no'. but a decision has been made and we must respect it. i should like to take this opportunity to congratulate mrs ulla sandbæk on her victory, because it is not just the stupid british conservatives and mr haider who have seen this as a good result.
- mr president, the result of the danish referendum should be understood without any false interpretations. the danish 'no' is not an isolationist or nationalist 'no'. this does not mean that a majority of danes are against european cooperation, nor does it mean that danes are not well informed about all the european union's affairs and plans for its development. a majority of danes have said no, as they did in 1992 and 1993, to greater integration in europe, no to the surrender of more sovereignty and no to the instruments that serve these purposes - that is, no to the euro, no to majority decisions and no to a european constitution. let me stress that the votes for the 'no' vote were cast by voters from across the political spectrum, including the ruling social democrats, who voted 'no' for half of them. it is not to be imagined that the european project, which we have heard president prodi so clearly describe, could convince the danes to vote yes. the danish government and the yes parties have tried with all their might to explain away the integration and the surrender of sovereignty that is coming. "the union is dead" is a phrase used to appease a people who have been independent for a thousand years. if the danish government had spoken as clearly as mr prodi and mr poettering have done here today, the 'no' vote would have been much higher. let me therefore say to you all: those who want more union must go forward alone, denmark and all free europeans who are allowed to hold a referendum will say no thanks.
the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities has adopted a report on the situation in the european union. - mr president, i join the chorus of those who say that the forthcoming enlargement is now the greatest task of the european union. if it doesn't work, we don't work. we can then have a good time in our little club, but the meaning of it all will be lost on the floor, we will not be able to secure the great prospects of peace, freedom and stability. but we will not succeed unless we understand that enlargement must first and foremost benefit not just some citizens but all citizens of the applicant countries - or at least most of them. and with all due respect to what is said and written in this house, we are not popular. that is why i listened very carefully to commissioner verheugen's words about 'mediating the eu' to the applicant countries, because most of the central and eastern european countries - if not all of them - have said that they want referendums or at least have not spoken against them, and i personally think that is a good idea, but you just have to be clear that you are undeniably risking a no. what problems did verheugen think the citizens were concerned about? verheugen mentioned migration, competition, social dumping, prices and so on. all of them, of course, but i think there is one thing that overshadows all the others: how can we survive as a country, a nation, a state? i am speaking, of course, partly because i come from a small country which has just voted 'no' in a referendum. no, it will now be analysed carefully in the future. there is no doubt that the right-wing forces have been involved, since we have also seen who paid for the ads. there has also been selfishness and, not least, populism, and the falling euro rate has certainly not helped in the right direction either. although many of us are aware of the historical background to the euro and the purpose of the whole thing, it has not been enough to convince. but all of this said, there are some considerations about how we can survive as a state that has taken in people. how can we avoid the fact that it is the politicians of the big countries like germany and france who are really in charge? i think it is extremely important that we keep this element in mind when negotiating with the applicant countries. i can assure you that in those countries the spectre of the union is very easy to conjure up. they've actually been trying it on their own bodies for a long time. another experience from denmark, which has direct relevance to the committee on whose behalf i am speaking, is that everything indicates that there are far more women than men who have said no. if we are not to risk a repetition in the applicant countries, it is therefore essential that we make it clear that this enlargement must not only benefit men but also women. i think that all the studies that have been done so far show that the transition to a market economy has affected women more than men. the traditional social values of the formerly planned economies of central and eastern europe have been put under very heavy pressure. the rapid restructuring of the old state-run industries which is currently taking place has resulted in serious unemployment among both men and women, while the private sector has not yet managed to fill the gap, and the figures indicate that there are a great many women who have become unemployed. even in countries like lithuania, where there is an economic upswing, it is not women who are allowed to occupy the jobs in the private sector, it is men. for some reason, it seems that men are the ones who really have the entrepreneurial gene. so we have to realize that women are much more vulnerable in this process of change, and therefore much more vulnerable in relation to the whole union project, than we have hitherto expressed. i would urge the commission to take this into account in all its negotiations with the applicant countries. if we do not ensure that women have jobs, if we do not ensure that ngos are on the ground to tell the people about the union project, then we can forget all about getting a popular 'yes', it will not succeed. that is why it is of course also important that we ensure that the equality legislation is implemented in a sensible way in connection with enlargement, so that the enlargement we all hope for - and i would point out that one of the countries where there is the greatest support for enlargement is in fact denmark - can really take place. but it does not come by itself, and i strongly urge that attention be paid to the situation of women.
- mr president, in the liberal group we have visits in these hours and days from future members of parliament from the candidate countries, and i can say that they are impatient. they see support for this gigantic peace project waning, both in the present member states and in their own countries. we must remember that it is not just for their sake that we have to expand, but also for our own. we're so busy figuring out what to ask of them and what to teach them. we should perhaps be a little more concerned with what they can rightly demand of us and what they can teach us. they are proud of their newfound freedom and democracy and their impressive economic growth, and remember, it was not us who gave them their freedom, it was they themselves who did it 11 years ago, when they astonished all diplomats and observers by seizing the opportunity to break the communist yoke and thereby also paving the way for german reunification. we think we should teach them about democracy, but do not countries that have experienced dictatorship know better than we do what is important in democracy? we need the new members from the east to help us to maintain the purpose of the whole thing, namely a union of free and sovereign member states, in which the freedom of citizens and countries is paramount. maybe they can teach us to use the proximity principle a little better. if there are rules that do not fit the new countries, then perhaps it is the rules that need to be adapted, not the new countries. if long transitional arrangements are needed on certain points, let us have long transitional arrangements. if we have to postpone the free acquisition of land and housing for family use, let us do it. the most important thing is to bring the new countries to the table so that they can help shape the future of europe. it's important to us, not just them.
in writing. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval, and the council will be able to accept it. we find it extremely reprehensible that the eu is in favour of setting the agenda for internal relations and links participation in economic and monetary union with the coordination of social policies. we refer in particular to paragraph 19 of the motion for a resolution in the report, which states that the european parliament stresses that the adoption of the texts of the provisions on economic and monetary union excludes any possibility of exit and represents a commitment to a comprehensive, irreversible political plan which necessarily presupposes real economic convergence and an approach, as regards emu, which does not mean mere membership of a monetary area but implies a high degree of coordination of economic and social policies.
in writing. - (da) the real political objective of the eu enlargement project is to subject the populations of the candidate countries to economic and political subjugation. the intention is to formalise a system that allows capital-rich companies in eu countries to dominate their eastern and southern neighbours while allowing the eu to transform itself into a military power. the danish people's movement against the eu has as its main objective the internationalisation of popular opposition to the european project as a whole.
- mr president, i should first like to thank mr cashman and mr mann for the two reports. it is of the utmost importance that we now ensure the implementation of the package which is the first and provisional implementation of article 13 of the ec treaty. we are thus taking an important step towards eliminating discrimination against minorities in the eu and in the applicant countries. i would like to stress two important points. firstly, i believe that if the anti-discrimination efforts we are now embarking on are to succeed in practice and in everyday life, it is absolutely essential that the ngos representing the minorities concerned are involved as much as possible in the work. otherwise, the effort will not succeed in the real world. the second point i would like to make is that, unfortunately, the commission has made a serious mistake in this first package by itself giving priority to the grounds for discrimination. only racial discrimination should be prohibited in all areas of society in general, discrimination on other grounds, such as disability and sexual orientation, should be prohibited only when we talk about employment and occupation. the grounds for discrimination must be equivalent. discrimination is equally offensive, whether it is based on race or sexual orientation, and i therefore strongly support mrs swiebel's proposal that the commission be obliged to correct this error within the next three years. i see the commissioner is here today. it is therefore a good opportunity today to confirm that this proposal will be followed up.
- mr president, mrs pack is right. this is about the principle of proximity. the eu faces a number of challenges - enlargement to the east, the intergovernmental conference, the treaty of nice. these are the topics on the agenda. the democratic problems and the problems of proximity to the citizens are other key issues. it is a question of the political legitimacy of the eu vis-à-vis its citizens - and it is getting a bit of a leg up on that at the moment. i consider the solution of these problems to be a prerequisite for the success of the european project as a whole. in his great speech yesterday, commission president romano prodi mentioned that for the sake of the political legitimacy of the union in the future it would be necessary to produce a catalogue of the issues to be dealt with by the union and the issues to be dealt with by the member states. i fully agree with the president of the commission that this constitutional catalogue should be drawn up, and it is quite clear to me that the principle of subsidiarity should be strengthened and that the eu should therefore refrain from making policy in the long list of areas where the political issues are not transnational. the eu must stick to the political main points. if the president of the commission has made up his mind about which areas belong where, i hope he shares my view that questions of parental controls on children's television consumption do not belong on the eu's agenda. i have therefore tabled, on behalf of the liberal group, amendments which are intended to limit the impact of this report should it be adopted. in conclusion, let me stress that the liberal group is also concerned about the increase in the supply of television programmes containing violence and sex. we are also convinced that these programmes are in no way beneficial to the users of the programmes, and certainly not to the children. but otherwise, i think it's the parents' job to keep their kids from seeing that stuff at home.
counsel. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, this is the third time since the beginning of the french presidency that i have had the pleasure of speaking before you, after a first hearing on 11 july by the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities and a statement on a monitoring system for industrial change on 5 september. i would like to speak today about the balanced participation of women and men in all areas of society. the motion for a resolution on the commission's reports, on which you will be voting shortly, is rich in this matter. let me mention three aspects of this which have been highlighted by mrs dybkjær, whose work as rapporteur before this house i commend. the first concerns the need for regular evaluation and monitoring of equality in all areas: work, employment, education, training, family life, health, women's dignity. only a thorough understanding of all the aspects which underlie discrimination will enable us to make significant progress. the second point concerns the new information and communication technologies. this emerging sector is opening up wider access to the world of work for women. we must be careful to reduce the gaps in this area. it is necessary to ensure that women benefit from induced jobs, while avoiding the same inequalities as in more traditional sectors: lower-skilled jobs, part-time work, sometimes unchosen, precarious contracts and difficulties in accessing decision-making positions. thirdly, i believe that proposing that the principle of equal treatment be applied as a selection criterion for all community aid or funding is essential for the promotion of equality. this theme of equality is a major political theme and this is how it will be expressed in several events during the french presidency. it is of course these demonstrations that i have come to discuss with you. we have organized a ministerial conference in paris. it will be held on 27 october. i have of course invited my distinguished compatriot mr rocard, as chairman of the committee on employment and social affairs, and mrs theorin, chairman of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities. we will also organise a european symposium on equal opportunities on 24 november, bringing together experts, social partners, business leaders and discussing the challenges of women's career paths. it is not so much on this symposium on employment equality that i shall be speaking this morning, but rather on the topics i have chosen for the ministerial conference on 27 october. three themes will be discussed and, moreover, we will find them in the major texts of the presidency, whether the fifth action programme or the social agenda. the balanced participation of women and men in decision-making, the balance between family and professional life, and the integration of equality into several formations of the union's council of ministers are the three topics on the agenda of the ministerial conference. the first, the participation of women in decision-making in all areas, shows how, even today, women suffer a significant deficit compared to men. it is a democratic deficit which is increasingly being tolerated. remember the paris conference in april 1999, perhaps some of you remember that moment, when we agreed on a declaration, but we had a very hard time agreeing, as 15 ministers, on an action plan with quantified objectives. that is why i have chosen this time to take advantage of the presidency's major texts to make progress on this action plan. i would like to say that my country has taken a decisive step forward in the field of women's participation in decision-making. i will be very brief on this subject by reminding you that in france the constitutional reform was adopted on 28 june 1999, supported by the prime minister and the president of the republic, and that, immediately afterwards, we debated a draft law on parity, adopted on 3 may 2000. this law will be applied to all elections and, from 2001, to municipal elections. political parties, at the risk of having their electoral lists invalidated or of not being reimbursed for campaign expenses, will have to propose lists with an equal number of men and women alternately placed to avoid relegation of women to positions offering them little chance of being elected. i am convinced that the balanced participation of women in political decision-making will bring about a real renewal of our entire political life. this sharing of power will bring about further profound changes in economic, social and cultural life. the beijing action programme and the latest recommendations of the un general assembly special session on beijing + 5 last june are in the same vein. this is clearly shown in the report drawn up by the finnish presidency on women and decision-making. the indicators it has developed will make it possible to assess the progress made. i would also like to welcome the initiative of the commission which, i am told - and i believe, madam diamantopoulou, that the information is correct - has taken a decision concerning the balance between men and women in the committees and groups of experts which it sets up. we must all be concerned to follow and develop this encouraging initiative. the french presidency is keen to make progress on this subject and would like to present to its partners proposals for quantified objectives in terms of women's access to decision-making in all areas: political - i have already spoken about this - but also economic and social. the first consultations initiated by the french presidency confirm that this issue is a major concern for all the member states. i am not saying that there is already an agreement on the quantified objectives, but i would like you to know that i am putting a lot of energy into reaching such an agreement. the second theme of this ministerial conference will be the link between family life and working life. as in finland, a questionnaire was sent to all member states and a report will be issued shortly. the council will be asked to submit a proposal for indicators to measure progress in this area. i wanted this to be an important item on the presidency's agenda, to ensure continuity with the work carried out under the portuguese presidency. i am thinking of the seminar in evora on motherhood and fatherhood and the resolution on the balanced participation of men and women in family life and work. because we know that as long as women have to carry the weight of a double day's work on their own, or almost, they will continue to be faced with a dilemma between their private and family life and their professional and civic life. i am even in the habit of saying in france, my dear friends, that while a law to impose parity in politics is still within the realm of possibility, it is more difficult to impose by law parity in everyday life and domestic parity. just as the public sphere must no longer be a male monopoly, the private sphere must no longer be the exclusive domain of women. new ways of organising time must therefore be devised so that all the parties involved - parents, children, citizens, elected officials, businesses - can find their place. this will be one of the highlights of the ministerial conference. and i'll add that i'm re-inserting this point into the november 24th equal employment conference. the third topic of the ministerial conference will be the integration of the gender equality dimension in the work of the various councils of ministers of the union. i would like to start a process that i have used as a working method in france and that works. for the first time, several councils of ministers will address gender equality by linking it to one of their agenda items. i will give you two examples: the education council with the point on the mobility of students and teachers, because we realised that there was a great inequality in mobility, and the internal market, consumption and tourism council with a point on the development of e-commerce to make it accessible to all, because, again, we know that there is inequality between men and women in the face of e-commerce. beyond this ministerial conference and the colloquium on vocational equality, two very important texts, specifically devoted to equality but coming under the employment and social affairs council, deserve to be mentioned. i am thinking of the fifth action programme and the revision of the 1976 directive. as regards the fifth programme, i would first of all like to thank mrs theorin and the rapporteur, mrs eriksson, very much for their diligence, which will hopefully enable the programme to be adopted before the end of the year. i know that parliament will pay particular attention to the mainstreaming and implementation of policy objectives which concern not only equality in economic and social life but also - i come back to this again - balanced participation in decision-making. i will, for my part, make sure that its funding is commensurate with our ambition. it is also in this sense that 25 years after its adoption, it is incumbent upon us to review the 1976 directive. this revision will not only bring the law into line with the many judgments of the court of justice but will also introduce other extremely important subjects into community legislation. i think of sexual harassment, for example, as discrimination based on sex. the concept of indirect discrimination will be more precisely defined and the exceptions to equal treatment better regulated. this is a particularly important step forward for women and one which i am sure mrs hautala will emphasize when she presents her report to this house on behalf of the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities. beyond the specific subjects within the competence of my ministry, i will mention three initiatives in a very synthetic way. first, the employment guidelines, which have been extended this time with quantified objectives. it is proposed that the employment rate for women should be 60% by 2010. the role of the social partners is also being strengthened, which i welcome. finally, the fourth pillar on equal opportunities reinforces the integrated approach to equality through gender mainstreaming and specific guidelines. the second initiative i would like to mention concerns the social agenda which will be submitted to the nice summit next december. it is a high priority of the presidency. as you know, it opens up a wide field of action in the field of employment and it makes a good point of mentioning that the combination of family and working life contributes to this. i would personally like to see this theme taken up even more concretely in the social agenda. i welcome - as you do - the fact that the agenda includes a chapter on promoting equality between men and women and i hope that we can move towards quantified targets. the last initiative i would like to stress concerns the drafting of the charter of fundamental rights. as you know, this project is the result of complex negotiations which culminated on 26 september last. the document will be submitted to the european council in biarritz in a few days. i would like to stress our commitment to the charter to include clearly the articles relating to equality between women and men. i'm often asked about this. i know that i have already used up my speaking time, so i will be very brief, but i would like to clarify a few things. three articles of the charter provide for equality between women and men. the first enshrines the general principle of equality before the law for all persons. the second concerns the principle of non-discrimination, including on grounds of sex. the third affirms the principle of equal pay and treatment, with the possibility of positive action in favour of the underrepresented sex. the right to family and professional life and the right to paid maternity leave, and more general rights such as the right to dignity and the right to physical and mental integrity are also included in the charter. all these provisions therefore make the text an additional instrument in support of equality. civil society expectations are high, and this was not lost on the convention members who wanted a "gender-correct" charter. the community level, in conclusion, i repeat, is the right level to make progress together in all the areas i have mentioned. but it's also the right level to progress on many other issues that affect women. i am thinking in particular of the violence suffered by women, one of the priority themes of my government's action, and i know that this theme also particularly moves you. any progress we make in one member state is progress for the other member states. while national policies are enriched by community policies, the latter must also draw on national experience. i am convinced that together, parliament, the council, the commission, but also the member states, the social partners and the non-governmental organisations, we will make progress and i know, and we know, that gender equality is on the move. male-dominated undertaking, and then we risk also in the applicant countries that we will get a negative vote and perhaps enlargement will fall. i would therefore urge the president-in-office of the council, both the present and the future president-in-office, and the commissioner to pay attention to this aspect, because i believe that it is absolutely fundamental to our work that we make a very great effort in this area. i should also like to say in connection with the reports - the president-in-office of the council was also aware of this - that one of the major problems in the european union is, of course, the great cultural differences. it is therefore no wonder that the president of the council is having difficulty getting his action plan through. it is clear that countries have very different interests, but one of the things we have to work on is trying to find out where we can specifically do something in each country. i have a dream that we can have proper statistics for recommend that this should also be done for the applicant countries. it is extremely difficult to obtain data from the applicant countries on the distribution of men and women in the various fields. i stop here. but i would urge both the presidency and the commissioner to continue along the lines we have indicated in the report and which i have mentioned in my speech. in writing. although we are in favour of a policy of regulation of european arms production and exports, we remain sceptical of the titley report for the following reasons: there is no link between the development of the arms industry and the eu's military and political cooperation. the eu has decided to create a common military competence in order to fulfil the so-called petrograd objectives (dealing with crisis situations and settling conflicts outside the eu territory), but it does not have a common defence policy. the eu member states are some members of nato and the western european union, while others belong to no alliance. the arms export policy must take this situation as its starting point and a distinction must be drawn between the control of politically motivated development of the arms industry (to deal with crisis situations) and purely commercially motivated arms exports, which in our view should be minimised and in the long term eliminated altogether. we particularly object to the wording in paragraphs ii and 1 (a) and (e) of the report, according to which the development of arms exports is an essential element for the development of a common defence policy and the shaping of a european security and defence profile within nato, and that the arms industry will benefit from the development of a common eu code of conduct on arms exports.
in writing. - (da) i voted against the report by mrs roberta angelilli on the commission communication on a study on parental control of television programmes. this decision does not reflect an opposition to the report as such, but a desire to send a clear and precise signal that the eu and the commission should focus on the essential tasks which are their responsibility. it is not the eu but the member states that have to take initiatives in this area. there are enough other pressing problems on which the commission should devote its energies.

- mr president, for the eldr group it is essential that the europe of the future should also be a social europe. the political task is therefore to find the best way to achieve this objective. it is important for us in the liberal group that decisions be taken as close as possible to the citizen. this is particularly true in the field of social policy. at the lisbon summit in spring, eu heads of government adopted a forward-looking vision for the development of a social europe. the emphasis was on promoting the eu's position in the knowledge society and on open coordination with common objectives but with flexibility and freedom in the choice of means. this was established as a guiding principle. the commission has found in its communication a framework for the social agenda which i think is very much in line with the spirit of the lisbon summit. we believe that open coordination is the right way forward if we are to secure a social europe. legislation may be needed in some areas, but we do not need a lot of bureaucratic and restrictive rules that discredit european cooperation without bringing any real results. i would also like to express my appreciation for the great effort that mrs van lancker has put into the preparation of this report. her work has been very constructive and when my group is sceptical about some of the main elements of her report, it is because we are politically divided, and that is fair enough. we therefore do not share the report's preference for legislation at eu level. there are a number of areas where we feel that too much attention is being paid to detail. i would like to stress that we in the eu countries have different traditions and different problems in the field of labour and social affairs, and the decisions of the lisbon summit recognise this. the commission's approach has been well balanced. technological developments and new organizational methods also favour flexible cooperation, where we focus more on objectives and the detection of results and less on rigid regulatory controls. such open coordination is not necessarily optional. no, it must be binding, of course, but it must be built from the bottom up and not developed by us centrally setting a lot of rules and quotas. open coordination must be open both in the choice of means and in the involvement of all those involved. it is therefore important to generate more public interest in and focus on open coordination. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i don't want it to be a secret. it must be much more widely known, and i think that parliament has something to offer in this respect. it can create greater publicity and bring greater public attention to the process.
- mr president, the commission has put forward a very ambitious proposal and i would like to take this opportunity to thank the commissioner for combining a great deal of understanding of national differences with a great deal of determination. without it, this would never have been anything but a nice piece of paper. i would also like to thank the other anna for her great work on this matter - we could not have done without it. the debate today and in committee shows that there are great differences of policy and great differences of ambition in this area, and i think it is good that these differences are clearly visible. the citizens will thus have a clear choice the next time they convene parliament. a choice between those who want a social europe and those who want the eu to regulate the single market alone. a choice between right and left. our liberal colleagues and especially our british conservative colleagues have put forward a number of proposals which will actually weaken the ambitions of this social action programme. every time legislation is introduced, these thatcherite-inspired liberals embark on an ideological crusade. but europe does not deserve dogma. europe needs us to make a pragmatic assessment of which regulatory instrument is the most effective in the given situation. in some cases we need the open method of coordination. other times, it is best for the parties in the labour market to negotiate an agreement. but we must not be afraid to use the law if that is what is needed to achieve our goals. i would also like to raise another specific issue which is the focus of this strategy. it is the need to make a determined strategy for the working environment. it is a matter of the european parliament's right to decide on the future of the community. and in the field of health and safety at work too, we must assess in concrete terms which instruments are best suited to solving the various problems. here too, dogma is not the way, but pragmatic solutions to real problems.
the commission is not - mr president, i very much welcome the commission's report. it is a very fine piece of work that commissioner monti and his staff have done in the past year. in my report on the 29th report on the commission's competition policy, i have devoted particular attention to the problems of mergers and the recovery of state aid. i've done that for a number of reasons. firstly, during the last year i have received several requests from undertakings which have felt a certain powerlessness in relation to the merger decisions, not least in relation to the cases in which they have had to appeal the commission's decision to the court of first instance. and as regards state aid, there appear to be still problems in ensuring that illegally paid state aid is reimbursed to national treasuries. but to start with mergers first: one consequence of emu is that we are seeing more and more mergers. and i think it is time to re-examine whether the regulatory frameworks and procedures we have today are optimal. the world is not always so easy for those who have to follow the rules and procedures that we help to shape. i think it is necessary to draw up clear rules for what is called the relevant market. it is important to distinguish between what can be called the peripheries of europe and what can be called the core of europe. it is not least important to get this in place before enlargement. the smaller peripheral countries may find it difficult when companies want to merge because they are rapidly becoming big in their own market. it is not very appropriate to prevent larger companies in small countries from merging, as this reduces their ability to compete globally. i would therefore like to remind you that we do have existing rules dealing with cases where companies exploit their large and possibly dominant market position. and that's why we may not have to be so afraid of mergers. after all, it's not a crime to merge. another point which is of particular concern to me is the need to speed up the processing of merger cases. i am thinking in particular of cases where a company wishes to appeal against a decision of the commission. there is simply no time in our global world that it can take between 25 and 35 months to get a case through the courts, where a big time-waster is the translation of the case files. we simply cannot be familiar with that. we owe it to our companies to provide the best conditions for them to plan their production and, above all, their future. i have two questions to ask you, commissioner monti, on mergers. will it be possible to consider different solutions to separate the judicial and prosecutorial functions in the commission? for example, during the second hearing, a panel of independent experts could sit so that it is not solely the commission's people who have to assess individual merger cases. i have another idea, although it may be a little outside your area of competence. but could not the chamber of first instance of the court of justice in luxembourg set up a committee of experts or a court of experts specialising in companies? for example, in denmark we have a special maritime and commercial law which deals exclusively with business matters. and then i would like to ask what the commissioner's explanation is for the fact that one of the most trusted staff in merger cases has left the commission. the second point i have mentioned in my report is state aid and the recovery of state aid granted unlawfully. it should be no secret that i am very keen to tighten up the rules on the recovery of unlawfully granted state aid. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. it is good that state aid to shipyards in europe will be abolished on 1 january 2001, although it is extremely problematic that our shipyards seem to have to continue to compete with the south korean treasury. in the light of recent developments, i can understand the commission's taking a harder line - and with good reason - towards south korea. i would therefore like to ask you, although i know that you have only a limited time, commissioner, to give a brief account of the very latest developments. i would also like to take this opportunity to ask commissioner monti when a public, efficient and well-functioning register of state aid can be expected to be set up and also when the long-promised scoreboard will become a reality. finally, i would like to point out that it is of vital importance to me as a liberal that companies are given proper and effective treatment in all areas so that there is no uncertainty about the future legal security of companies. and then i would like to thank my colleagues in the committee for the very good cooperation they have shown in my report.
- madam president, the biarritz summit was a breakthrough in enhanced cooperation. the core is that the most inclusive elites will be able to make decisions without broad popular support. it is no longer necessary to take account of those countries where the surrender of sovereignty requires a referendum. in future, proposals can be rejected by referendum and adopted anyway. the countries that say no will be forced to follow suit sooner or later. the model is known from judicial cooperation. first, the pioneer countries implement schengen cooperation, and then the legislation is later transferred to all countries. the most stubborn countries get a special offer. they can have the legislation faxed to them without interference, but they cannot avoid the legislation, no matter how many people vote no in the referendum. elites in every country are also uniting against their own constituents. those outside the enhanced cooperation will in effect have the same freedom as the backseat passengers in a car. enhanced cooperation is like an aloe vera plant. there is no turning back, only ever more integration, more centralism, more brussels without the democracy that goes with it. the edd group wants a freer and more flexible europe, where there is no pressure to be part of everything, with democracy instead of uniformity. a europe of democracy and diversity.
the council. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, thank you for your welcome. it is not without emotion that i enter this magnificent chamber, full of so many symbols, for the first time, and it is an honour for me to take part in this part-session of the european parliament, which will no doubt examine and adopt, at first reading, the community budget for 2001. it is also a unique opportunity for me, since florence parly, the french state secretary for the budget, who is the president of the budget council, was unable to be present today and asked me to apologize and represent her. the budget debate is an important moment in parliamentary life. the budget is also essential for european governments and citizens, since it sets the broad guidelines for european action in 2001. i was able to appreciate the quality of the consultation last week in brussels, at the trilogue, and so i am returning to you today. i would like to point out the excellent atmosphere in which this budgetary procedure began. the meetings we have had, at least one of them, together have been useful and have allowed for a better mutual understanding of our respective priorities. this partnership between the council and parliament on budgetary matters deserves to be deepened. i hope that the budgetary work will continue in the same spirit, so that we can reach a budget for the financial year 2001 which will enable us to respond to the priorities and challenges that the european union will face in the near future. you are called upon today to give your opinion on a budget which has already been amended by the council at first reading. i will therefore briefly remind you of the main guidelines which the council wanted to set for the 2001 budget. i would also like to stress the objectives which the council and parliament share and which i believe can be translated into an agreement between the two arms of the budgetary authority. on 20 july the council adopted a draft budget with a 2.8% increase in commitment appropriations and a 3.5% increase in payment appropriations compared with the 2000 budget. the community's proposed budget, which will increase by 3.5% in 2001, is much larger than the member states' national budgets allow. this is also much higher than the inflation forecast by the commission for 2001, plus 1.8%, which reflects the council's commitment to financing eu policies. this draft budget is a balanced and realistic compromise. the council did not make a tactical first reading, which would have consisted in adopting a hard line on stabilizing community public expenditure, in anticipation of a contentious budgetary negotiation with parliament. i think that attitude is outdated. the council voted on the appropriations for 2001 on first reading, which it considered justified, without taking account of the tactical concerns which serve the objective of the proper allocation of appropriations to be pursued by the community institutions. the council's draft budget makes it possible to reconcile the financing of all the union's policies and priorities without placing an excessive burden on the member states, which, i remind you, are engaged in a process of controlling their public expenditure. by giving the council room for manoeuvre, particularly in respect of the ceilings for heading 3 and heading 4, the council also intends to enable parliament, which has final jurisdiction over this non-compulsory expenditure, to express its priorities without calling into question those of the council. i shall therefore simply emphasize the highlights of the council's draft budget. firstly, the draft budget makes it possible to identify a priority effort for the balkans, with an increase of 30% in the budget compared with the year 2000. the €614 million voted by the council will cover all the foreseeable needs and includes an initial €40 million in aid to serbia. the council has shown that it is possible to grant substantially increased amounts to the balkans without a revision of the financial perspective. the council also shares the view of parliament that the revision as proposed by the commission is unthinkable on another point, namely the reduction of expenditure on agricultural markets. the council reiterated that the ceiling for cap expenditure was not being reached. secondly, all the other public priorities of the union are financed by the common agricultural policy, which is being increased by 6.3% to finance the reform of the cap. structural measures are stabilized at a high level. all programmes adopted jointly by parliament and the council shall comply with the programming agreed jointly. i would like to remind you that the employment initiative is a priority of parliament, but also of the council, in the wake of the conclusions of the feira european council. the commission's assessment will have to be awaited, but we will make every effort to reach agreement with parliament at second reading in the context of a more comprehensive compromise on the budget as a whole. let me now briefly refer to the council's vote item by item. on the cap, which is heading 1, the substantial increase in appropriations of over 6.3%, as i said, is the consequence of the berlin agreements which reform the cap. the council reduced the amount of appropriations for 2001 below the commission's proposal, which provided for an increase of 7.6%, in particular because of the traditional under-implementation of agricultural expenditure every year. eur 660 million of under-performance in 1999. i would like to remind you that the increase in cap expenditure is compensating for the fall in agricultural prices for consumers and, immersed as i am in the french agricultural programmes, i am today appreciating the effort you have made to reach this agreement and to meet the commitments that all european farmers expect. the cap reform also improves the position of european agriculture in the wto rules. in the long run, this reform will bring agricultural costs under better control. the council's position on the savings of eur 225 million in rural development takes account of the accumulated delays in the national rural development plans. the commission acknowledges these delays and there is no doubt that they will have an impact on the implementation of appropriations in 2001. however, expenditure under this heading rose by 4.6%, reflecting the priority given to the second pillar of the cap. the amount of structural measures has been increased overall compared with the 2000 budget. i would remind you that the 2000 budget provided a very large amount for the structural funds. the council also carried out a rebalancing of this category of expenditure in favour of the cohesion fund: 360 million for the cohesion fund, compared with the preliminary draft budget, was taken from the structural funds, in view of the forecasts of implementation transmitted by the member states. as regards internal policies, the council has set aside a greater prudential margin for heading 3 than the commission had proposed. while the commission proposed a margin of €137 million below the ceiling, the council raised it to €208 million. the council is not voting for additional savings, but for a margin of appropriations sufficient to enable the amounts relating to the priorities shared by the council and parliament, such as the employment initiative, to which you attach great importance, to be adopted by the second reading of the budget. in the case of major multiannual programmes such as the rdp and the framework programme for research and development, the council respects the timetables for appropriations approved by parliament. as regards external action, heading 4, the council adopted a budget which respects all the union's priorities, in particular aid to the western balkans, which is an unquestionable priority for the union. the council nevertheless considers that this priority must not lead the union institutions to depart from the principles of effective and credible intervention. analysis of needs, adaptation of levels and modalities of assistance to the nature of these needs, allocation of efforts among all bilateral and multilateral donors. the council did not consider it desirable to adopt the programming proposed by the commission, either in the proposal for a framework regulation or in its preliminary draft budget for 2001. the council estimates that an allocation of eur 614 million, representing a very significant increase of 30% compared with 2000, will cover this priority, especially as the aid arrangements will change from emergency aid, which is by definition used quickly, to more structural aid, the commitment of which is generally more complex. this effort makes the european union the leading contributor to the reconstruction of the western balkans. i would remind you that this effort is part of the larger framework of all the aid given by the various other donors and financial institutions. the meda programme and the euro-mediterranean partnership as a whole are of course also a priority for the union, and the saving of eur 150 million in commitment appropriations proposed by the council does not call this into question. the level of commitment appropriations is not a problem for the meda programme, since, as commissioner patten has said, it has been more than eight years behind schedule. the improvement of the management of this programme is therefore the council's main concern. the increase in the actual payments of the meda programme and the implementation of projects on the ground will depend on this improvement. agreement was reached in the council and with the commission on the arrangements for managing this programme. only the multiannual amount remains to be defined. i would like to stress, however, that meda is only one aspect of the euro-mediterranean partnership, which includes other measures and requires reciprocal efforts between the partners. the improvement and deepening of this partnership is also a priority of the presidency of the council. the council's approach to heading 5 is to stabilise current expenditure at the level of inflation, while at the same time providing additional appropriations for exceptional expenditure by the institutions. as regards the commission's letter of amendment, which proposes the creation of 500 new posts, which have just been mentioned, in 2001 the council voted to create 400 posts. the council wished to give the commission a favourable opinion, recalling the internal reorganization effort it was making in accordance with the white paper proposals. however, the council does not intend to issue a blank cheque to the commission since the early retirement mechanism and the creation of posts for the coming years have not been validated. a more detailed discussion with parliament will have to begin after this first reading on the subject. as regards pre-accession, heading 7 is the heading in the 2001 draft budget with the highest increase, almost 11%, reflecting the priority given by the council, and shared by parliament and the commission, to this category of expenditure. before you comment on the draft budget for 2001, i should like to stress a number of objectives which the council and parliament share and which can be translated into an agreement between the two arms of the budgetary authority. i will take two examples: the ral and the financing of aid to the balkans. these are two subjects which are often raised in discussions between parliament and the council and which could, if not dealt with carefully, lead to tensions between the two arms of the budgetary authority. i am convinced, however, that parliament and the council, although they seemingly have two different approaches to these issues, have common objectives and interests which can be translated into an agreement. the rest of the money, the ral, first. this term means that appropriations which have been committed in previous financial years have not yet been paid. there is therefore a lag between the authorization to commit expenditure and its actual payment, which is normal in the case of financing infrastructure or multiannual programmes, in particular. however, the prevalence of alr has increased to such an extent that its cause has to be sought elsewhere. the first answer, which your committee on budgets suggests you adopt, is that if there is too much ral, there is not enough payment appropriations. the solution would therefore be to increase these payment appropriations substantially to cover all outstanding commitments. this is a voluntary solution, but it is ineffective. the community budget does not need more payment appropriations, the community budget has never been in a payment crisis. the commission has been able to demonstrate this by the fact that each year it finds that a large amount of appropriations has not been used. in 1999, eur 2.2 billion of appropriations were not used, and nearly ecu 3 billion in 1998. the increase in payment appropriations beyond the 3.5% decided by the council is therefore not necessary to reduce the gni. you have the credits, this increase is useless. the answer to the ral is rather a change in approach. the amount of commitment appropriations voted should be better adjusted to real needs and to the capacity of the beneficiaries to absorb or use the appropriations, programme management should be improved, as we propose for meda, and the system of commitments should be reformed in the context of the reform of the financial regulation. i hope that the house will take these few remarks into account. the commission, the council and parliament are all trying to achieve the same objective of making the community budget more efficient. however, there is a need for common reflection on the causes of alr and how to remedy them. i'm here to help the balkans now. the recent and positive developments in serbia make this a priority for the union's external relations. this priority is shared by the council and parliament, which have just agreed on emergency aid of €200 million. i think i can say that the union has effectively financed all the needs for the balkans for the year 2000, since the community budget has made it possible to respond quickly to the acceleration of the reconstruction timetable for kosovo in particular. as regards the next programming of heading 4 of the budget, the council wishes that a serious needs assessment can be carried out to confirm or refute the political figures for the cards programme. the council wishes, as far as possible, to use a technical analysis of needs as a basis for defining the overall multiannual budgetary envelope, including that for 2001 for the reconstruction of the balkans. i come now to an important question which is the subject of much discussion in the corridors of parliament and the commission: should the financial perspective be revised to help the balkans? the question was already raised in the interinstitutional agreement of 6 may 1999. i sometimes hear that the council is so attached to berlin's financial perspective that it does not even want to hear about a possible revision. that is not the question. others, on the contrary, say that the commission and parliament believe that the revision is symbolic of the balkans and furthermore proof that berlin's financial prospects are not sufficient. i guess that's also excessive. the council's attitude to the revision of the financial perspective is calm. the council is committed to all the provisions of the interinstitutional agreement, including the financial perspective and the possibility of revising it. i would simply remind you, however, that a revision of the financial perspective is conditional on three preliminary steps. the first is the needs assessment; the second is the analysis of the margins available within the framework of the existing ceilings; and the third is the possible mobilisation of budgetary instruments prior to the revision such as the flexibility instrument. i would like to reiterate that at this stage the council does not consider that the revision of the financial perspective is necessary to finance reconstruction in the balkans. i would like to conclude by saying that we should not give the financial perspectives the symbolic value they do not have. the council intends to spare no effort, in particular through a significant budgetary allocation, to meet the needs in the balkans. the budgetary envelope for the western balkans by the union will be symbolic, but not whether it is financed through a revision of the financial perspective. there is no advertising effect in this matter. if aid to the balkans can be financed under the financial perspective, the main thing is that it is actually financed. at the end of this brief overview, i would like to remind you once again that the council wishes to maintain the close cooperation with parliament which was established at the beginning of the budgetary procedure. the draft budget already incorporates many of the lessons learned from the consultations between the council and parliament. the debate will now continue in your institution with the council and the commission. i hope that these many exchanges will make the 2001 budget a good budget.
the commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, mr president of the council! the commission's proposal for a regulation on the common organization of the market in bananas is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done. the requirements for the european budget for next year are considerable. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i am glad to say that the programmes for rural development have finally been approved, which means that this new programme will be fully operational next year. we are in a similar situation in structural policy. the programmes for the new 2000-2006 funding period have now been approved for almost all the countries, so we can expect and hope that next year the first projects of the operational programmes will be implemented, and this also requires appropriate budgetary resources. i would remind you that in the field of research policy, the challenge of supporting small and mediumsized enterprises is that in lisbon the heads of state and government set themselves the very ambitious goal of making europe the most competitive knowledge-based economy in the world, and that also places demands on the european budget. in the field of enlargement, we have the situation that there are also new programmes to promote pre-accession aid and reforms. the date of enlargement is hopefully approaching, but this also means that the pressure for reform is approaching for the candidate countries, and therefore pre-accession aid should of course also provide corresponding assistance. first of all, in foreign policy we have - and this has been stressed on several occasions - new challenges and, quite simply, new opportunities, namely to have improved relations with our neighbours, especially in the balkans, to promote stability in the balkans. these are the enormous challenges facing european policy, the budget, and the administration of the commission which is responsible for implementing it, and here too we need increased resources. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. what we are asking of the member states also applies to the european budget, and bringing all this together is what makes budgetary policy so exciting, as everyone who is involved in it knows. this requires balancing the various interests, it requires negotiations between the institutions, and we are now at the stage of the discussions on the 2001 budget, which means that for all the institutions the first ideas and decisions on the 2001 budget are on the table, from the european parliament, the council and also the commission in its preliminary draft budget. if you look at these three different figures, you can see that there are still considerable differences, both in terms of quantity and, of course, in terms of content. let me just say this briefly: the commission has proposed to use 96.9 billion in commitment appropriations, the council 95.9 billion, the european parliament 96.8 billion, and as regards payments, there is a difference of almost eur 2.2 billion between the parliament's decision and the council's decision, so that the gap to be filled is relatively large in terms of quantity. but i should also like to stress that the proposals and decisions of all three institutions for the 2001 budget are below the limits set in the financial planning in berlin. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. i must point out that the berlin decisions would allow an increase in payments of 6.1% compared with the previous year, and no such increase is contained in any decision. that means that there is still a lot of need for negotiation, and anyone who has been present at the trilogue last week, the trilogue between the european parliament, the council and the commission, can confirm that a good solution is really being sought and that this is a real atmosphere of negotiation and not an atmosphere of dispute. i should like to thank parliament and the rapporteurs, mrs haug, mr ferber, mrs rühle and mr colom i naval, in particular. i would also like to thank the council and in particular mr patria for the brilliant performance of last wednesday in the trilogue. the summer consultation - mr colom i naval pointed this out - also had a good result, namely that improved information will be made available to parliament in future on legal bases with an impact on expenditure. i can fully support your proposals, mr colom, on behalf of the commission, because the committee on budgets of parliament has a great responsibility and the task of keeping track of the fact that the committee on budgets does indeed need these new instruments to help make decisions that are financiable. it should not happen in the council either, for example - as has recently happened - that the agriculture ministers reject a commission proposal for austerity, that is, the agriculture ministers vote for higher expenditure and then the council of finance ministers reduce agricultural expenditure. i must therefore point out that the decisions must also have sufficient funds. it is also necessary to ensure that the backlog does not build up but is reduced, but that the commission also has the necessary human resources. the commission has therefore decided to present a requirement for 400 new posts in this budget. i would like to point out once again that we did this only after we had critically examined all the departments to see if there was any potential for staff redistribution, and only after that did we come to the conclusion that we must approach the budgetary authority with a request for new posts. i should like to thank the council for its positive vote on this matter. parliament has now put the posts in reserve, and i must of course fully accept, mrs haug, that conditions will be imposed for the release, but i ask you to take into account that there must be conditions which we can also meet. the conditions for changing the comitology procedure were set out. i should like to point out that, after a long debate last year, a new decision was taken with the council on the comitology procedure, and i have little hope that this debate can be reopened. the same applies to the condition that a performance reserve be provided in advance. if we want to get better, and that's our desire in performance, then of course we need the jobs. we will do our best to explain to you what steps we have taken in reform, where we are and what is planned, and to show that the commission is making every effort. i must also ask parliament to be prepared to negotiate here. the main challenge for budgetary policy makers next year, or at the moment, is to ensure the financing of new foreign policy tasks, and this concerns above all the question of financing the necessary stabilisation support in the balkans. i would remind you that the budget for the balkans adopted last year provided only 1.85 billion for the whole period up to 2006. it was a decision taken under completely different european political conditions than those which we now have, fortunately, with the change of power in belgrade. i would also remind you that last year, for example, there was a long debate with the council on the reconstruction measures in kosovo: will we be able to do this at all, since we are delivering a good performance? the implementation of the reconstruction aid to kosovo by the agency for reconstruction is very good, so that we are in a position to increase the budget for the agency for reconstruction by 175 million this year, and i should like to thank the committee on budgets and in particular its chairman, mr wynn, for supporting these measures in this way. the increase in aid to kosovo this year makes one thing clear above all else, and i think it is very important as a political message that the european union does not say: because there has been a change of power in serbia, we are cutting aid to kosovo. no, it is not likely that kosovo will suffer a second time. on the contrary, it is necessary that we fulfill both tasks. the question of financing serbia for next year and the years to come is of course on the table. the question of whether the necessary measures can be cut out of the present approach to the mediterranean programme is a political question and not a technical one. the berlin financial plan for the period up to 2006 provided for eur 8.1 billion for the meda programme. if you put it at five or six billion, it's not the same policy to finance. the question that must be answered is, of course, what policy do we want to pursue in our southern neighbours on the part of the european union? the commission does not believe that the necessary reconstruction aid for the balkans can be entirely excluded from the scope of the meda programme. we have various instruments for budgetary policy when unforeseen tasks or additional requirements arise. this is the flexibility reserve. i have noted that the council says that it is ready to negotiate. i note that the european parliament says: the flexibility reserve should also be used to compensate for the cuts in the meda programme. i would like to point out the following: the flexibility reserve is an instrument available to finance an unforeseen task in one year. the question of the necessary support in the balkans is not a one-year task but a multiannual task. we also have the negative reserve instrument in the budget. there is also the instrument of increasing expenditure in category iv with the corresponding compensation over the whole budget, and we have the instrument of revision or modification of the financial planning. the interinstitutional agreement does, of course, lay down certain conditions, but it does not stipulate that the flexibility reserve must be used first. it's not in the text, but there are certain steps. the commission has also gone along with this, and i can say again that this would give us a guarantee for the whole period. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has proposed this budget to finance the necessary expenditure on foreign policy and thus to meet the challenges. i would ask you once again - the commission leaves its proposal on the table - to examine this proposal impartially and to consider it as a possibility. the european union promised serbia, before the elections, that it would provide immediate assistance in the event of a change in the balance of power and a return to democracy. we stand by our word, and we will all stand by our word together. the commission had proposed to activate the emergency reserve and to provide a package of aid. this package of 200 million euros is intended to be used this year to take the necessary measures in the energy sector so that people have the necessary heating energy in winter, to alleviate the emergency situation in some areas of medical care, for example to ensure that children do not sit in school in winter freezing, but to finance necessary repairs. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i believe that the european union has really demonstrated that it is capable of acting together very quickly, that there is a common will on this point too, a common will to use all its strength to ensure political stability and peace in europe.
