advisory rapporteur to the committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy. - mr president, it was nice to hear the commissioner's criticism of the council in terms of foreign policy. when we see a dictatorship in the balkans being replaced by the hope of democracy, we cannot be locked into budgetary disputes, the budgetary framework - the so-called financial perspective - must be adjusted. we can't just take all the money from other parts of foreign policy. we have already exceeded the budget for foreign policy by 400 million this year. let us now be farsighted and give the commission the opportunity to plan. the event also demonstrated the need for the so-called 'rapid reaction facility', i.e. the rapid reaction instrument proposed by the commission. the money must not be put into the reserve at the risk of the council blocking it, which would mean that the commission could not plan. remember romano prodi's big speech here a few weeks ago. we must support his and chris patten's efforts to strengthen the commission's capacity to act by means of parliamentary oversight and the necessary parliamentary scrutiny, which the council and mr solana are not subject to. so i would ask both the council and the committee on budgets in this parliament to think in terms of foreign policy and not just budgetary policy when they deal with foreign policy.
- mr president, i have to go. this time, when presenting the budget for the coming year, the commission has chosen a new way of presenting the figures. instead of just showing expenditure broken down by account, the budget in the new format shows more clearly what we get for the money. we'll break down the expenditure by policy area and by activity. it is a good innovation, which has more than just an educational purpose. the educational aspect is that we should all be able to see better what we get for our money. as an elected representative, you are naturally pleased with any step which can make the budget more understandable. but when spending is allocated by activity, it also makes more sense to see the budget as a political instrument, with the distribution of spending reflecting clear political priorities. in the spring, commissioner chris patten illustrated this by asking the question: 'why is the eu allocating more money to clean up after hurricane mitch in latin america than it has allocated to developing the russian economy?' these are the kinds of questions we should be asking ourselves when we study the budget. because those are the questions our voters will rightly be asking. the new structure has made it easier to see and understand the whole picture, but the new structure, activity-based budgeting, must also reflect the reform process, which is to establish a simplified commission with delegated management and a clearer allocation of responsibilities, which is a somewhat more difficult matter. there are major organisational changes under way and a new culture is needed, with a genuine willingness to take responsibility for the implementation of the various parts of the budget and an acceptance of the consequences of failure to live up to that responsibility. i wish the commission every success in these endeavours. the political messages in this year's budget are - as commissioner schreyer has already mentioned - many. the reform of the agricultural policy, new structural programmes, ensuring better implementation of structural policies, following up the decisions of the lisbon summit to create a stronger and more competitive eu and combating social exclusion. these are important issues, but the political issue that deserves the most attention of all is foreign policy. there is a great need for eu support in both the third world and balkan applicant countries. in the middle of the budgetary procedure, milosevic was forced to relinquish his seat, and the many promises of financial aid to serbia must now be fulfilled. we all agree - the council, parliament and the commission - that we must be frugal and we have all kept well below the ceiling on expenditure. so the discussion about how we get the money to serbia is not about a lack of will to stay within an economic framework. i do not think our voters will be able to understand the budgetary technicalities of why we cannot agree to find the money for serbia and the balkans without deteriorating the effort in other countries. it is not a popular demand that there should not be a revision of the financial perspective. on the other hand, it is a popular demand that we find a solution and support serbia. and let us find a multiannual solution so that we do not have to go through this discussion again next year and the year after that about how to find money for the reconstruction of the balkans.
- mr president, landmines are an unimaginable evil whose purpose is not to kill but to cripple and create the worst possible pain and burden for the victims and their families and their countries. think of the soviet toy bombs in afghanistan! unfortunately, they're infinitely cheap to produce and insanely expensive to find and destroy. it'll take 100 years to get rid of them, and more are still spreading. it was the liberal jan willem bertens who raised the issue five years ago in this house, and i think this house can really be proud of having started this case, so that we are now moving towards a mine ban. and so we can point to the very large and effective effort that the union is making on this matter. but more can be done. there are machines that can clear large areas of land of mines in a short time. i've seen a machine like this in kosovo, but it was sitting idle and not being used. mrs bonino and mr patten are right that it is obvious to destroy the stockpiles because it costs only one hundredth of what it costs to destroy a stored mine compared to what it costs to destroy one that is placed in a place where it is difficult to find it. mrs bonino and mr patten are also right that parliament and the council should keep their fingers off the administration, and i listened very carefully to mr patten's remarks on this point, because he obviously thinks we should keep our fingers off even more. i agree with the principle. we must refrain from interfering in the administration. we should instead criticise the commission if it does not manage properly. but we're not gonna do it by remote control. this is a mistake that this parliament is constantly making. the commission must have the freedom to exercise its powers, and then we must have the freedom to fuck it up - if i may use that term - if it does not do its job well. finally, i would like to thank mrs bonino. it is a charity to have such an experienced rapporteur. thank you for a good report.
- mrs bonino, one minute's speaking time for a report which really shows your commitment is very little, but fortunately i agree with most of the conclusions of the report, including the implicit criticism of the countries which have not yet ratified the ottawa convention. unfortunately, my own country was too long in doing so. there is a problem with the report. in my view, there is a tendency to try to empty the cfsp of civilian tasks. i think it is healthy to strengthen the civil obligations of any organisation that has military activities. i think that the real demand on the council and on the cfsp and the member states is that, in addition to normal development and humanitarian activities, any sum spent on military activities should be matched by at least an equivalent amount for civilian activities, such as demining in the areas concerned.
in writing. - (da) the danish social democrats voted in favour of the report on the fight against anti-personnel mines. in its communication the commission sets out the actions in which the community has already taken part to combat the use of antipersonnel mines. denmark has repeatedly participated in humanitarian mine clearance operations, whenever they were civilian and non-military, in order not to contravene the derogation granted to denmark in the field of defence policy. the measures were financed by appropriations from various budget lines. the commission therefore proposes that an initiative be taken in favour of a horizontal budget line. parliament will reject this solution if the present structure, with its many budget lines, is not transparent and does not contribute to greater coherence. the legal basis which the commission will propose for this initiative is not yet known. it is therefore difficult at present to predict to what extent the initiative might conflict with the derogation obtained by denmark. it would be unfortunate, however, if denmark were not able to take part in a project to eliminate these terrible and inhuman antipersonnel mines completely in the next 10 to 15 years.
in writing. - (da) while we agree with the main intention of the report, we do not always agree with the means described to achieve it. we are concerned that the cfsp may be deprived of its responsibilities in the field of civil actions. a sound security and peace policy would be better guaranteed if the appropriations allocated to the cfsp and the resources provided by the member states for military action were accompanied, in identical proportions, by an increase in appropriations for humanitarian action.
in writing. (sv) unfortunately, the european parliament's committee on constitutional affairs has not dealt with any of the events that have taken place in the european union in recent years. in the 1999 european parliament elections, voter turnout was only 40% if we exclude countries where voting was compulsory and countries where national elections were held at the same time as the european parliament elections. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. now, in september 2000, the people of denmark voted against their country's membership of emu and certainly would have voted against emu and the citizens of many member states had they been given the chance. we have noted with interest how the danish social democrats cheered on the night of the emu elections when the results of the elections in the aristocratic areas were announced, while they showed their disappointment when they saw how the working-class areas voted. this alone should awaken many consciences to see the bad texts in the development of the eu. the committee on constitutional affairs, despite the defeat of the plans for a federal eu, has presented us with yet another report which aims at the creation of a european constitution, despite the fact that our citizens are in no way asking for this. what is really needed is a clearer definition of the 'principle of subsidiarity' in the european union. today, this principle is merely a tool for the european union, since the union itself decides at what level the various political decisions will be taken, with the result that the union absorbs one policy area after another. there is also a need to simplify the rules on flexibility so that cooperation at european level becomes ever easier, as the people of europe want. the european union is not involved in the negotiations, and the areas in which sovereign member states can take decisions should be clearly identified.
the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your kind words. i would of course like to thank you for your constructive contributions to the debate on climate change and how we should combat it. in just a minute i shall comment on the floods that have struck italy and spain. i shall read out a statement from the commission; i shall come back to that shortly. first, however, i would like to conclude the discussion on what we intend to do in the hague in a few weeks' time by saying that we must of course continue to play the role of international driving force. we will have to be the ones to take the initiative, the ones to push forward and at the same time the ones to build bridges between, not least, developing countries and countries like the united states and others in that group. we must of course force the reluctant to make this climate change conference a success, and show that we are credible in action too. to answer a direct question, i do not think we will succeed in the long term unless we have access to a range of instruments and a range of policy measures. it is, of course, about transport and our transport systems, which must be equipped for the future, and it is about energy policy. i am, however, pleased that the commission's initiative in this area has been recognised in this respect. i think we need more economic instruments. we must also discuss new areas and the integration of environmental and climate considerations into all policy areas. again, this is about credibility. emissions trading is just a complement to the measures at both member state and european level that we are looking to take in our fundamental political systems and infrastructures to address climate change. i think it is right, as has also been pointed out here today, that we are looking at competitiveness and the socioeconomic impact. however, this problem must not be used as a reason not to trade, but may even be turned to our advantage, into something that strengthens competitiveness and gives us advantages. once again, i would like to thank the rapporteur and all those who have taken part in the debate. of course we will come back as soon as possible after the hague conference with a plan for how we are going to implement all these measures. i hope that we will have the rules clear and settled then. i hope we can come back from there and say that it has been a great success.
the commission is not - madam president, we have today a considerable body of knowledge on endocrine disrupting substances. there have been many suspicions, but many causal relationships have been established as to how endocrine disruptors may affect humans and animals. we know it can cause a decrease in reproductive capacity. we know that there can be deformities of the genitals, both in animals and in humans, and we know that they can cause serious diseases, including testicular cancer and breast cancer. however, there is still considerable uncertainty about the overall impact of the many endocrine disrupting substances. the situation is therefore in fact uncertain, even for future generations. i think that's a pretty scary prospect. the commission has been very careful to ensure that the commission does not take any action against the member states. this situation is obviously untenable. it is therefore good that the commission has come forward with a communication on a community strategy on endocrine disruptors. i simply think that the commission's approach is a little too forward-looking. it could be a little more ambitious. i think that more aggressive and effective initiatives are needed in the short term. and that is what i am trying to correct in this report. first of all, it is important that the list of substances which are or are suspected of being endocrine disruptors be drawn up and that this be done very quickly. in the report i have proposed that this should be done before the end of the year. it is also important to have the means to ensure that the list will include all the relevant substances, probably about 560. at the same time, it is necessary to identify the most dangerous substances on that list so that action can be taken immediately on the most dangerous substances. these measures may be different: prohibitions, phasing out over a shorter or longer period of time and other restrictions on use. this list of dangerous substances should be ready by mid-2001. in this connection, i think it is important to state that this must be done without waiting for further testing. we must therefore do so by using the precautionary principle offensively, and i think it is also important to pay attention to the particularly vulnerable groups. i am thinking of pregnant women, i am thinking of infants and certain groups of workers who are particularly at risk of being affected by these substances. i also believe that a great deal of effort must be made to establish effective monitoring in the member states and at eu level. the industry must be required to report on the chemicals placed on the market, the quantities used and their expected effects. we all know that more resources must be devoted to research into both the scale and the consequences, as well as research into test methods and, in particular, research into the effects of mixing and synergies. we know that these drugs can interact in a particularly unfortunate way. i also think that in this connection, specific research on risks is important and that we should also ensure that the research is independent, that is to say that we do not base our policy initiatives on the producers' own research. we also know that international cooperation is important in the field of research, including with the united states and japan. as part of future legislation on chemical substances, i think it would be good to have a specific framework, specific chapters for endocrine disruptors. there must be a legislative framework based on the precautionary principle and the principle of reversing the burden of proof. at the same time, a timetable should be established for testing existing substances. when i refer to a legislative framework, it is also to say that i believe that the rules should be binding. all experience with voluntary agreements in this area is poor. i would like to thank the rapporteurs of the various groups and the commission for the cooperation we have had in drawing up this report. as regards the amendments, i should just like to point out that mine is a linguistic improvement on paragraph 15 of the report and that i cannot recommend that we vote for mrs grossetête's three amendments. i believe that the proposals are largely inspired by the chemical industry, which has approached me personally. the intention is clear that they will try to block any intervention here and now, and i think it is important to stress that the advice we receive from the scientific ethics committees is advice and does not become actual legislation. we in the commission, the council and parliament are the ones who have to set the legislative framework, not the scientific committees. they serve as advisors in the same way that we receive advice from the other side.
- madam president, i would like to thank mr torben lund for the excellent report he has produced, which deserves the full support of the whole of parliament. the effects of man-made chemicals released into the environment are clear. there are many examples of animals and plants that have been impaired in their ability to reproduce and develop. one of the more frightening examples of this is the sexual dysfunction observed in bears in antarctica. this example shows that the harmful effects of various endocrine disrupting substances are a global problem, affecting even the most isolated parts of the world. the research results presented by the danish professor skakkebæk on the marked deterioration in the quality of men's sperm should also be of great concern. we're actually at the point where young men should be getting tested for their sperm quality to see if they should start a family before they've even thought about it, just to be fairly certain of having children. the precautionary principle should therefore be placed at the top of the agenda, as torben lund rightly said. it is now that we have the opportunity to apply this principle more aggressively. in the light of the precautionary principle, we will be able to introduce emergency measures to avoid possible irreparable damage to humans, animals and plants. it is now obvious that we must put the fine words of environmental and health protection into practice and apply the principle in a field about which we know very little. it's the only answer to an uncertain public. the report's recommendations that future framework legislation on chemicals should be based on both the precautionary principle and the reverse burden of proof cannot be followed up fast enough.

- mr president, all the countries of the european union agree to combat money laundering, and that agreement must of course be unconditionally welcomed. but the reality is not so simple. i think that this parliament is complicating the problems rather than simplifying and making them more efficient, and this is reflected in the proposal to extend europol's powers. the assembly should give priority to the creation of genuinely effective instruments to combat money laundering and professionally organised crime. the reaction to the initiative taken by finland is both regrettable and instructive. it is clear that cooperation between the countries could lead to a better fight in this area. why is the commission's new system considered necessary? in my view, it is not clear where to go beyond the imposition of uniform data protection rules on the member states. in other words, the fight against money laundering should not be hindered by the fact that the commission must target its efforts on the most lax and indifferent member states. parliament should not block the initiatives of the council of ministers and those of the countries most committed to stopping these crimes. parliament should encourage the council of ministers and the member states. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the criminal offences are defined in a uniform way and that the police and judicial authorities of the member states cooperate.
- mr president, i would like to join the few who dissent and warn against the ideas, plans and projects contained in this stream of legal reports which are before us today, including mrs gebhardt's report. it is very precisely described in the explanatory statement that what is happening at the moment is the institutionalisation of judicial cooperation, or rather the tendency to make it transnational. it is, as the report very accurately states, 'a seed for a future european public prosecutor's office, strengthening the judicial authority of the european union in criminal matters' and it refers to the drafting of a european criminal code with its institutions, police, prosecutor's office, judiciary, etc., which was adopted in connection with the wiebenga report in 1999. but there are two areas in which they are fundamentally wrong. firstly, the over-nationalisation of criminal law itself. the criminal justice system is one of the most visible cultural expressions of a society. it is the legal cultures that are at stake here, because there is not one european criminal law and not one european criminal policy ideology - there are fifteen. and the incorporation - the institutionalisation - of these projects into the various bureaucracies of the european union creates an opaque, elitist system which in many respects is at odds with basic democratic ideals and the rule of law. the second point is also interesting. the european parliament has been a very active participant in the process of european integration. interestingly, the title reads: 'establishment of eurojust - a rule of law imperative'. what rule of law are we talking about? has the eu become a state? it is indeed on the way to achieving this, because it is precisely the ambition, as it is so precisely expressed in the last paragraph of the first part of the explanatory statement, that the european union should establish bodies with a 'balanced and independent division of legislative, executive and judicial powers', that is, the characteristic of a state. for these two reasons, we cannot expect any cooperation from my group.

- madam president, mr dupuis and i are not talking about the same text. the vote on the charter is a deliberate attempt to create a first part of a constitution for what could be called the united states of europe. although the treaty will not be incorporated until a special constitutional conference in 2004, the court of justice will have a new source of judgments when it is signed with the commission, the council and the european parliament, and its representative has already announced that it will be inspired by the charter. the commission representative at the turin convention attributes legal significance to the document. it is therefore absurd for the danish government to claim that there is nothing new in the charter. what were we supposed to do with the lyrics? of course there are new things in the charter compared to existing law, such as a ban on discrimination against national minorities, which is good, and there is a new wording on positive discrimination of the underrepresented sex and new rules on cloning and personal protection and the right of access to documents. the problem is not the content but the aim, which is to surpass national constitutions and the european declaration of human rights. the court of justice of the european communities will be our new supreme court and constitutional court, also in all matters of fundamental and human rights, and it will also be the supreme interpreter of the european declaration of human rights. i have tabled amendments in the convention to make the charter conform to the national constitutional courts and the court of human rights. it could not be adopted, and that is a testimony to the very purpose of the constitution. i am therefore unfortunately obliged to vote against.
- mr president, i can pick up where mr gahrton left off. i think it is a problem that at the same time one claims that social rights must be indivisible and universal, and then at the same time consistently, in the section called 'solidarity', refers to national legislation and practice. this is a weakening of the very universality and indivisibility of these areas. that's the part of the content i'm having trouble with. but i'm also having problems with the construction we're working on. if we really want to strengthen human rights at european level, we should instead devote our energies to strengthening the work of the council of europe, to having the eu ratify the european convention on human rights and the corresponding conventions on cultural and social rights, instead of slowly building up a competing system with respect to the council of europe, as we are now doing. the council of europe represents the whole of europe, all the european states. we only have fifteen - yet. finally, there is the problem of wanting to build a constitution without popular support. it will not be able to win the vote in the next european parliament elections.

in writing. - (sv) in chapter vii, general provisions, article 51.2 of the draft charter of fundamental rights, it is stated: 'this charter does not create any new competence or task for the community and the union and does not alter the competence and tasks defined by the treaties.' that is why it really lacks impact on citizens' rights. the debate on the charter drew enthusiastic applause from supporters of a federal europe, who want to see it as an embryo of a constitution for the european union. we see a real danger looming with the adoption of this charter. it risks undermining the work of the council of europe on human rights and leading to a different approach to human rights in european countries in the future. we shall vote against this charter because we reject it in general. this does not mean that we are against human rights themselves. but we believe that these rights can be guaranteed by other bodies than the european union. we consider the charter proposal to be a bad text which it would be reasonable to leave aside. we call on the european union to ratify the european convention on human rights and other conventions on social and cultural rights instead.
in writing. - (da) although we are in favour of the fight against money laundering and we agree with the need to equip the competent authorities with the appropriate instruments to enable them to achieve this objective, we cannot vote in favour of the marinho report. in so far as this report, like the karamanou and roure reports on which the members are also to vote today, provides for the approximation of the criminal procedures of the member states with a view to establishing criminal frameworks for money laundering, an indirect step is taken towards the creation of a community judicial system with a court of justice, a police force, judges, a judicial organisation, etc., which we reject. the fight against money laundering is, in any case, carried out at various levels and we do not believe that there is a need to define criminal frameworks for this type of crime at community level.
in writing. - (da) the unveiled intentions of the gebhardt report to institutionalise and supranationalise judicial cooperation by creating eurojust are based on a particularly problematic premise. there is not one but fifteen different traditions of judicial policy, just as there are fifteen different judicial systems within the eu. the idea of embedding these different legal traditions in eu bureaucracies could lead to an elitist and opaque system, which would not enjoy any popular support. such a system would not be able to solve the problems facing the member states. we are also strongly opposed to the idea of making the eu a state governed by the rule of law through the establishment of a 'balanced and independent coexistence of legislative, executive and judicial powers'. it is the member states, as the rule of law, which must continue to be at the heart of european cooperation. that is why we shall vote against the report.

council. - the tragedy in dover last june has made it clear that we must deal more effectively with the channels of illegal immigration. the feira european council last june asked the council to speed up the implementation of the measures to combat illegal trafficking provided for in the conclusions of the tampere european council of october 1999. the measures referred to by the author of the question consist of a draft council directive and a draft framework decision, the first of which aims to define the concept of aid for entry and irregular movement and the second of which aims specifically to strengthen the criminal framework for the repression of aid for entry and irregular stay as defined in the directive. these texts, which have been sent to the european parliament for consultation, are currently being examined by the relevant council groups. they are essentially aimed at combating trafficking in human beings, through the aid to illegal immigration through organized channels. the aim is to be able to dismantle and sanction criminal organisations that are involved in this type of trafficking. the honourable member's question therefore gives the presidency an opportunity to clear up some confusion. the texts concerned are not intended to prevent potential refugees from exercising their right to apply for asylum in the territory of the union. the honourable member will agree that a potential refugee is not ipso facto an illegal immigrant. nor are these texts intended to prevent non-profit-making organisations from carrying out their task, provided, of course, that they comply with the applicable laws. so it is a question of fighting criminal organizations involved in human trafficking and not of dismantling non-governmental, charitable, political or religious organizations, unless, of course - but i cannot even imagine it - their activities were used as a cover for illegal activities.

- mr president, i would also like to thank mr rocard for his own-initiative report, although i did not agree with his original intention, which was to establish supplementary solidarity health insurance schemes in all the member states of the european union with a common set of rules for such schemes. but i can support the commission's presentation of a green paper on health insurance, with a particular focus on health insurance and crossborder activities, to see if further initiatives are needed. health insurance is a national responsibility, the systems are different, but it is true that the increasing number of elderly people and the growing demands on the health sector mean that the systems are coming under economic pressure. it is a problem that calls for many different solutions, including better organisation and efficiency of health care, so that better quality is obtained for the same money, and new medical methods can actually also lead to both better and cheaper treatments. i am generally in favour of money following the patient wherever possible, so that the patient and not the system is put at the centre, and in that respect, health insurance-like schemes and supplementary insurance schemes can certainly have their justification. but each country must decide for itself what it prefers. it is wrong for the eu to make rules on, for example, health information or to require insurance for everyone, thus destroying the diversity of supplementary health insurance schemes that exist today in many countries and operate on actuarial principles. it is true that in the case of serious illnesses such as cancer and aids there must be a compulsory and solidarity-based system which ensures good treatment for all. supplementary health insurance is intended to be supplementary, for example for those who are willing to pay for an additional service, or to cover the costs of more banal and frequent ailments for which actuarial calculations can reasonably be made.
the commission mr president, it is true - as i said before - that this is one of the points we have raised in the german case, where there are two points of interest. on the one hand, the umts licences - as i said earlier - have been devoted to debt reduction, which allows the public debt to be practically at the level of 60%. for accounting reasons, the revenue from third generation mobile licences also has to be taken into account for the deficit reduction. for this reason, the resulting budget for the year 2000 in the german case will result in a surplus situation of one and a half points of gross domestic product. if we deduct from this one and a half points the revenue from mobile phone licences, we are left with a budget deficit of 1%, which is the figure we need for the stability programme. so we will have a situation - to answer your question - of a deficit of 1.4% in 1999, a deficit of 1% in 2000 and - as you say - in 2001 the deficit rises again, instead of falling, to 1.5%. why does the commission accept this increase in deficit reduction? the commission considers that the measures are imputable to the state and that they are imputable to the state. germany has a higher than average tax burden. the fiscal measures taken by germany affect the supply side of the economy more than the demand side, will have a certain impact on demand and we believe that they will allow the growth potential of the german economy to increase in the medium term. we therefore consider these measures to be positive, despite the fact that they represent a step back from the previous situation. however, and we leave that very explicitly stated in the programme, we are particularly concerned about this backsliding and we are aware that these backslidings have to be recovered later. we therefore urge the german government to take special care in drawing up the work programmes for 2002 to increase revenue and reduce expenditure so that the budget deficit can be reduced again. under the current programme, the budget deficit would fall to 1% in 2002, to 0.5% in 2003 and germany would be in budgetary equilibrium in 2004. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

- mr president, combating social exclusion is a political aspiration which we can all support, but what is social exclusion and how can it best be combated? what is the eu's role? the answers to such questions are to be found in this programme to promote cooperation between member states in combating social exclusion. the idea is to involve all the parties concerned and interested in a work in which each country describes the extent of social exclusion and its efforts to combat it. this provides the basis for a more common framework of understanding. it is not so easy to define the concept of social exclusion. when we think of the longterm unemployed - young and old - do we think of the physically or mentally handicapped, do we think of the mentally ill, do we think of the poor or the underprivileged? i think we all have our own ideas about what should be included. but we do not have a common definition. the commission tried to define excluded persons in its report to the lisbon summit as those whose income is less than 60% of the average income in the country concerned. the governments of the member states did not accept this definition, which is a bit simplistic and too meaningless. we need a more detailed description of the problems. the very open approach to the question adopted by the council is, in my view, quite correct. there is a need to collect statistics, to make analyses and, through conferences and other dissemination, to create a greater common understanding of what we understand by social exclusion. as regards the specific report, mrs figueiredo wants the word 'poverty' to be inserted in the legislative text. my group is generally opposed to this on formal grounds, namely the question of the treaty basis. we recognize, of course, that poverty is a source of social exclusion, as the text also states. i think we can all agree on that. some members of the eldr group also have reservations about the inclusion of the reflection group in the legislative text and the increase in the appropriation, but otherwise we can support mrs figueredo's report.
- mr president, like other speakers, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on the very comprehensive and good report which contains a number of proposals which should inspire and be adopted by decision-makers around the world. the problem with such a report, however, is that it does not give priority to its many proposals. that was not the intention either, i'll admit. but we also run the risk of simply moving on to the next item after the vote. i would therefore suggest that we prioritise our next step and start with what we can do concretely in terms of putting into practice the build-up of civil and military preparedness that is taking place in the eu in these very months. i therefore call on the council and on solana and the commission, including chris patten, to look at the many ideas in the report and to take inspiration from them and to bring these ideas and proposals into the work that is going on right now before our eyes, and where we unfortunately know very little about how it will actually unfold. it can be a very good test of what all the words about equality, mainstreaming, empowerment of women, etc. mean in practice. let's start with concrete action. the council and the commission should then come back to parliament with a statement on what could be done. there are many obvious things to be addressed, such as training of police officers or training of military personnel, which are certainly grounds for improving the understanding that rape is indeed an offence which cannot be accepted, just as we cannot accept torture. it could also teach the involvement of ngos, constructive facilities for people in crisis, etc. we could then hold hearings, seminars, etc. on these subjects. in addition, we would have transparency about the whole process and the structure of these preventive measures, and if this could be done on the basis of involving women, then we might suddenly and unexpectedly have given the european union a human face in a single area.

- mr president, we have spoken so much in this parliament about bringing the union closer to the citizens, and i think that if that is what we really want, this project is the most important. it is a than for something similar to escape from the swedish government or possibly from the danish government. that is why i think it is so crucial that the rights of citizens in the member states which now have good access are not restricted, but that cooperation is used to improve access for citizens in the individual member states and, indeed, in the union as a whole. so if we are serious about citizens' rights and about bringing the union closer to the citizens, it is not about fine documents and big speeches, it is about very concrete rights for the citizens.
- mr president, the commission's position on access to documents can be seen in the internal manual which was sent to commission staff on 11 october to tell them how to deal with requests for access to documents from members of parliament. it was sent to parliament on 13 october, but i have only seen it tonight. it states that if a member of parliament requests information in the context of the discharge procedure, mrs de palacio's staff must refuse to answer such requests. there is only one person - the chairman of the committee on budgetary control - who is able to request information in connection with the discharge procedure. it is also clear from this internal staff manual that if a member finds an interesting item in a newspaper and wants to see the document in question, only one member of parliament can make such a request, and that is the president of parliament. it is a scandal and it shows what the commission intended to do with the framework agreement. if even members of parliament cannot get the information they ask for, how the hell are ordinary citizens supposed to get it? and that's why there's a good reason why all members go to their groups... - mr president, first of all i would like to thank mr cashman for a very fine work on this report. i think that the report sends a strong signal to all the eu institutions and to the public. transparency in legislation and public administration is the very foundation of the functioning of our democracies. it is therefore not enough for a system to be formally open. it's supposed to feel that way. the commission proposal for a regulation is in fact reminiscent of the famous fairy tale by h.c. andersen about the emperor's new clothes. you say one thing, but you do something else. we must take on the role of the little boy in the fairy tale here in the european parliament. the usual way of working together internationally with all the customs and rules of diplomacy, including everything being done behind closed doors, does not fit into the modern cooperation that the eu has developed. we must put this habit of thinking to rest. we must have full public access with very few exceptions, otherwise we will lose the support of the people for european cooperation. and i would add that, although the rules here only concern the public in the eu, mr cashman's proposal will also have a spillover effect in the member states, where documents will no longer be able to be kept secret on the standard justification we almost always encounter, namely that it is a european matter. the discrediting of the eu in the public eye that lies in this attitude will be brought to an end by the cashman report. and this is good for citizens' confidence in european cooperation. and i should just like to conclude by saying that i think it was a pity that mr jens-peter bonde had the wrong manuscript for his speech today. it certainly did not concern the cashman report.
in writing. - (da) the danish social democrats in the european parliament voted today in favour of the report by mr michel rocard on supplementary health insurance. the report is based on the finding that an increasing proportion of health insurance in europe is covered by supplementary health insurance schemes. this is a serious problem if it implies that there is discrimination against the population on the basis of financial capacity and health status. this problem was brought to light during the debate on the report in the committee on employment and social affairs following the comments made by our british conservative colleagues that in the field of insurance the setting of the premium depends on the risk taken by the insurance company. for example, a woman who has an inherited predisposition to breast cancer will have to pay a higher premium than other women. this is a breach of the very fundamental principles of solidarity on which the european social model must be based; the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this field, and it is therefore necessary to take a joint initiative to avoid any exclusion of such complementary systems. the report by mr michel rocard stresses that health insurance is primarily a member state prerogative and that the principle of proximity must be applied. the european parliament is asking the commission to report on the policy of the member states precisely because we have a common interest in ensuring that all citizens in the member states have access to high quality health services. the better the health services offered by the member states, the less the need for supplementary insurance. this is an area which should be addressed through an open method of coordination, such as that which has been implemented by the european council to combat social exclusion.
- mr president, the draft treaty of nice is more a constitution for a federal state than an agreement for a federation of states. as a general rule, the laws of the union must be adopted by qualified majority, so that all the democracies of the individual countries can be voted against. there are still 65 provisions left in the amsterdam treaty with unanimity. it is now proposed that decisions be taken by qualified majority in 49 areas. so we are at the end of the road, if not a united state. the competences conferred on the eu by the treaty of nice go beyond those conferred on the federal level in many federal states. and now we are entering the heart chamber of the nation states with majority decisions on aspects of social policy, labour market policy, taxes, industrial policy, culture, supranational parties and even our own pay, so that we become the highly paid representatives of the eu in the member states instead of the taxed representatives of the electorate in the european parliament. the commission will be responsible for the implementation of the budget. trade policy makes the eu countries look like one state. we need to speak with one unpleasant voice in the wto. foreign and security policy must also be adopted by majority vote. only on the defense should there be continuing consensus. otherwise, the veto power in the new treaty is small, and countries without the veto power are more states than independent nations. majority decision-making at home, joint representation abroad - that is the recipe for a state. what is left for the 2004 constitutional conference is not the content but the discretion of a text that may also come to resemble a real constitution. that is to say, a text of the charter of human and fundamental rights written in first, then the rules of the game and finally an annex with the individual policies, which can be changed without difficult ratification. the only thing missing is democracy. democracy was forgotten along the way. and that is the essence of the maastricht, amsterdam and nice treaties. more majority decisions where officials and ministers can vote down voters and elected officials. in democracies, it is the other way around. majority decisions kill the veto power of the parliamentary democracies of the member states, but the eu does not become a parliamentary democracy itself. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the federalists can get their way with more integration, and the governments of sweden, britain and denmark can lock in the countries once the decisions are made and still bind in practice. the nice treaty leaves so much power to the electorate that it should be treated as a genuine constitutional change and only come into force when approved by referendums in the countries where this is possible.
- mr president, i was part of the active danish 'no' to the maastricht treaty in 1992. i will take a position on the treaty of nice when i see the result, but it is a somewhat schizophrenic experience to listen to the debates here. one minute we are talking about bringing the union closer to the citizens, the next we are building a european state that is out of step with what the citizens want. the most important thing at the nice summit, in my view, is to set out a roadmap for when and how the eu will be ready for enlargement. we focus too much on the readiness of the applicant countries and we too often ignore the fact that, for example, the eu's agricultural schemes and structural funds are a huge obstacle to enlargement. a decision in nice, for example, that the applicant countries should be allowed to take part in the next european parliament elections, could help to put the necessary pressure on the eu itself so that enlargement can become a reality.
in writing. - (da) the danish social democrats in the european parliament voted today in favour of the joint motion for a resolution on the preparation of the european council to be held in nice from 7 to 9 december, which will mark the end of the current intergovernmental conference with the conclusion of the treaty of nice. we have expressed our opposition throughout the intergovernmental conference to the idea, supported on many occasions by a majority of members, of substantially broadening the agenda; we have also stated this position in every vote on the motions for resolutions tabled on this subject. many of the motions for resolutions tabled by the various political groups are based on a deep concern as to whether the objective of accession of the new eu candidate countries can be achieved in nice. the main task of the nice summit will be to ensure the effectiveness of the eu institutions. to achieve this, a balance must be struck between small and large countries, not only in the weighting of votes in the council but also in the composition of the european parliament and the commission. these two institutions are very much concerned with enabling the eu to continue to focus on common objectives and crossborder tasks while other issues remain the responsibility of the member states. enlargement is a vast project that requires the support not only of the candidate countries but also of the countries that are already members of the eu. many meps want to constitutionalise the treaties, including the charter of fundamental rights and the use of qualified majority voting in all areas. we believe that it would be unreasonable to insist on these points at the present intergovernmental conference, which already does not enjoy wide support, and that this would contribute to depriving the assembly of its influence. we have also stressed this point in the various votes today. we have decided to vote for the report as a whole because we want to put pressure on the heads of state and government to achieve a satisfactory result in nice.

in writing. - (da) the danish social democrats in the european parliament decided to support the report on the establishment of a common european security and defence policy. we have reservations, however, about certain aspects of the report. it should be stressed, first of all, that in view of the derogation obtained by denmark in the military field, denmark will not be able to provide personnel for the establishment of a joint european intervention force. secondly, we are sceptical about the idea of establishing a real airspace policy if the aim is to equip europe with a defence system similar to that which the united states is establishing. a common european airspace programme could, however, have advantages for research purposes as well as for environmental monitoring. thirdly, the report contains a number of points which express the wish to give the european parliament greater influence in the formulation of a common european security and defence policy. we believe it is essential to stress that the formulation of a security and defence policy is a problem which must be resolved between the states and in which the european parliament does not have to interfere. we cannot therefore approve any further institutionalisation of the common security and defence policy. it is up to each member state to determine the extent to which it wishes to participate in a common european security and defence policy. secondly, it is not for the european parliament to define the capacity that a european intervention force should have and whether, in this connection, the intervention capacity should be maritime and airborne. we also disapprove of the european parliament's wish to oblige all member states to contribute financially to the european intervention force even if a country does not participate in an operation, because this could, for example, lead some countries into a conflict in which they do not wish to participate. we also stress that no harm can be done to neutral member states whose wishes for neutrality must be respected in all respects.

- mr president, the broadly agreed message from the president of the council, the commission and the heads of government is that the treaty of nice provides the framework for european unification. some are happier than others, but the main message is clear: europe can now come together in an enlarged democratic union, which is also close to its citizens, thanks to the treaty of nice. these messages contain such massive and monstrous distortions of reality that we have to go back to the most famous propagandists in history to find the right comparisons. the fact is that what was decided in nice is enhanced cooperation, the dominance of the great powers, the charter of rights, democratic monitoring and military build-up. what has been decided is not necessary for the sake of enlargement, and what is necessary for the sake of enlargement has not been decided. the treaty of nice contains a series of decisive steps towards the union of the elites and corresponding steps away from the europe of democracies and peoples. central power and the dominance of the great powers are being increased tremendously, as if we had not already had enough. is this the unification and democratization of europe? no, it is an extension of the power of the elites, and the unpleasant agenda of reality for eastern europe is called submission. we are in charge of the applicant countries. the eu does not negotiate with them. they are not involved in the decision-making process of the treaty of nice, and the eu dictates all the conditions for participation, and all - i repeat - all the crucial problems associated with the enlargement project, the integration of incompatible social and economic systems, are not recognised as problems. the eu and eu policy are not the solution, but the problem.

in writing. - (da) we voted in favour of the report proposing the introduction of new instruments to substantially improve the protection of the community's financial interests. we believe, first of all, that the idea of supplementing the olaf regulation and creating a unit within olaf's remit is a good one. this unit will be responsible for conducting investigations and facilitating criminal follow-up before national courts. the unit will deal only with criminal acts committed by eu officials. such a prosecution, under the responsibility of olaf, is necessary because experience shows that criminal monitoring of olaf's work is far too often lacking. it is often not clear in which country a case is to be dealt with, and the complexity of the burden-sharing procedures often leads to a statute of limitations or the closure of the case. unfortunately, cooperation between states is not enough. the conventions concluded between states with the aim of combating fraud are not ratified. five countries have not yet ratified the 1995 convention on the protection of the eu's financial interests. in the longer term, therefore, it will be necessary to examine the possibility of introducing a european public prosecutor's office responsible for criminal prosecution in cases where member states are accused of fraud against the community's financial interests. at present, the member states are not doing enough to combat fraud against the community's financial interests. on the other hand, 80% of fraud cases are cross-border. legal proceedings are often difficult to conduct. there are major differences in the definition of unlawful acts and the penalties to be applied, as well as in procedural acts, rules on the administration of evidence and the powers of the public prosecutor's office in matters of control. it is feared that the problem will only get worse with the removal of borders, which has given organized crime even more freedom to operate. mr president, i would like to say that the commission has been very careful in its analysis of the chinese dumping, as we must, of the damage caused to european industry and of the possible consequences for users. the investigation has concluded that there is no evidence of dumping and injury and that the effect of the anti-dumping measures on users is limited. the employment question was given special attention. the coke industry employs a total of 70,000 people, but coke represents only a very marginal share of its costs, around 2%. it was also found that its users could pass on a moderate price increase to customers. consequently, negative effects on employment and competitiveness in the coke industry of the user industry are unlikely. on the other hand, the coke industry itself, which employs 1,900 people directly in the manufacture of coke, has deteriorated so much under the chinese dumping that site closures are now being considered. the price increase resulting from the anti-dumping measures should allow the coke industry to regain profitability. therefore, in view of all the arguments put forward, it was concluded that the imposition of anti-dumping measures is in the overall and general interest of the european union. as regards the environmental part - which is not part of the anti-dumping investigations but which must nevertheless be seen in a broader context - i would like to mention that community environmental legislation sets emission limits which must be respected by both producers and the user industry, regardless of the origin of the coke, and therefore, whether it is chinese coke or coke from elsewhere, the environmental consequences are the same. this investigation was conducted in accordance with the usual procedure. i believe it is fair and transparent and all the arguments put forward by the user industry have been duly taken into account, as usual.
- commissioner, i can understand that you do not want to change your decision, despite the fact that ten member states are against such a tariff. i very much disagree with you, mr lamy. as much as i agreed with you this afternoon on the korean shipyard policy, i disagree with you on this matter. i think it is important to stress that we are dealing here with a coke industry with only 800 employees, but we have a user industry with over 6,000 employees, which is not very competitive with such an anti-dumping duty. but i can understand that you still disagree with me, so i think it is not in my interest to ask you again, but i think you should take into account that ten member states are against such a tariff.
- mr president, the environment and public health are like a complex machine with many functions, which we monitor and control in order to intervene so that nothing goes wrong. for the machine we use a lot of control lights that warn us and tell us that if one lamp is red, then something is wrong and we must act immediately so that the machine does not break down. we cannot wait until more or all of the lights are red. that is the precautionary principle in practice. but that's not how it works in public health. we wait to intervene until all the lights are on, and that is the problem with the european commission's communication. the precautionary principle, in my view, simply concerns the right and, in particular, the duty to intervene by adequate and necessary means in the event of a suspicion or presumption that a substance or process may cause irreparable damage to the environment and to health. those who cannot live without the substance or process in question must prove that it can be done without causing lasting damage. the commission proposes, inter alia, to examine the benefits and costs of action or inaction in a given area. but that is exactly what was done in the bse scandal, and that is why the disaster struck england and now france, which had all the indications of bse and yet would not use the precautionary principle. a benefit-risk analysis is an absurd idea when you think about the idea behind the precautionary principle, which is to be able to react to a product before you know the full benefits and disadvantages. the same applies to the commission's requirement of a prior in-depth risk analysis. if you know all the risks, costs and so on, then we do not need the precautionary principle. i also think that too much emphasis is placed on the assessment of a case by experts, toxicologists and economists. risk management and the enforcement of the precautionary principle are not just a matter for experts but a matter for politicians in general, and the commission's communication does not provide sufficient incentives for politicians to live up to that responsibility.

in writing. - (da) the danish social democrats voted today in favour of the report by mr werner langen on the state of world shipbuilding, because we believe that it is important to establish a system of fair competition in the world shipbuilding industry and that the way in which countries like south korea grant state aid to their shipyards is likely to deal a fatal blow to the world shipbuilding industry. unfair competition can contribute to the deterioration of the quality of the shipbuilding work environment, to a reduction in technical knowledge and to job losses and, in the longer term, to a monopoly situation, which would be detrimental to world trade. we cannot, however, approve the concrete proposal to extend production aid for two years. we do not really think that we will be able to solve the problems of the shipbuilding industry by maintaining competition on the basis of the volume of state aid. our decision to refuse a two-year extension of production aid is, however, subject to the political condition that fair competition also exists within the eu. the commission must therefore follow very closely the decisions taken and take action if it finds that illegal state aid is still being granted to shipyards within the eu.
